- **Credit where due: the benchmark prints the row a competitor wins.** On a *single machine* **Cyclone DDS beats Zenoh's Rust core, 8 µs vs 10 µs**, and the authors give the mechanism — DDS uses UDP multicast and Zenoh's main implementation did not. Zenoh-pico, which does, lands at **5 µs**. The ordering inverts across a real network, which is the case that matters, because multicast is exactly what fails across subnets, over Wi-Fi, and through NAT.
- **The robotics numbers are 5 bytes and 300 bytes, not 4M msg/s.** 5-byte wire overhead (it can run on OSI Layer 2) and a **300-byte Zenoh-pico footprint on an 8-bit Atmel** mean one protocol addresses a sensor MCU *and* a cloud store — the thing DDS has not done well and the reason ROS 2 fleets split into "the DDS part" and "the cloud part."
- **Queryables are the primitive with no ROS 2 analogue** — a registered computation that fires on query, collapsing RPC, map-reduce, and historical retrieval into the same key-space operation as a live subscription. Directly relevant to the [fleet framework](syntheses/projects/fleet-agentic-framework.md) and [ros2-mcp-server](syntheses/projects/ros2-mcp-server-design.md) work.
- **Filled the remaining §6 lint gaps**, prioritizing what the project plans depend on: **[RTAB-Map](entities/rtab-map.md)** and **[GTSAM](entities/gtsam.md)** (the navigation leg of the [XLeRobot plan](syntheses/projects/xlerobot-nav-manip-teleop-bringup.md)), **[CuRobo](entities/curobo.md)** (the planner under RoboTwin, and the reason a 5-DoF arm returns *residual orientation error* rather than a clean "infeasible" — the detail that shapes the [5-DoF experiment](syntheses/projects/five-dof-arms-in-robotwin.md)), and **[LangGraph](entities/langgraph.md)** (the agent inside DimOS's `McpClient`; its cycles and interrupts are the machinery the [across-stacks synthesis](syntheses/agents/llm-agent-architecture-across-stacks.md) found missing everywhere).
- **[DP3](entities/dp3.md) earned its own page for one result**: it beats every VLA on RoboTwin Easy (**55.2**) then collapses to **5.0** under randomization — and the benchmark's own authors concede the clean number *"partly stems from perfect point clouds and clean background segmentation in simulation."* The wiki's clearest case of a **simulation artifact flattering a policy class**, reported by the people who built the benchmark.
- **Filed a holding page rather than five near-empty stubs** for the [LIBERO tie cluster](entities/libero-tie-models.md) — CogVLA, VLA-Adapter, MemoryVLA, FLOWER, BAKU. Each is currently one number and one inference; five stubs would add link targets without adding knowledge, while one page makes the shared fact explicit: **the wiki cites a ten-model statistical tie it cannot characterize.** Highest-value primaries there are **MemoryVLA** (held the 7 B Simpler-WidowX SOTA that 0.9 B X-VLA beat by 23.9 pts) and **FLOWER** (CALVIN 4.53 — the one benchmark X-VLA lost).
- Lint after the batch: **951 pages · schema 0 · broken links 0 · wikilinks 0 · orphans 0 · index coverage 100% · dates 0 · source-count drift 0 · stale stubs 0.** Only the ~320 one-way citations remain, open by design.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | Moondream — the last §6 gap closed
- Created [moondream.ai](sources/moondream-ai.md) + [Moondream](entities/moondream.md). **The lint punch list's §6 knowledge gaps are now fully closed.**
- **M87 Labs' Apache-2.0 tiny VLM** — 9,963★, 5M+ monthly downloads. Moondream 3.1 is a **9 B sparse MoE with 2 B active**; the **2 B dense Moondream 2 is what the ecosystem actually runs** (2.39 M HF downloads vs 3.1's ~12.6 K). Five ops on every tier: Query · Caption · **Detect · Point** · Segment.
- **It earned a full source page for one reason: it publishes a nine-tier hardware latency ladder that runs down to Jetson** — exactly the measurement the [control-rate ladder](syntheses/platforms/control-rate-ladder.md) and [deployability landscape](syntheses/platforms/vla-deployability-landscape.md) keep finding absent. **Thor 246 ms ≈ 4 Hz; AGX Orin 514 ms ≈ 2 Hz** → the **perception/planner band**, above frontier LLMs (0.2–0.4 Hz), near [SmolVLA](entities/smolvla.md) (1.4 Hz), far below the [ACT](entities/act.md) control tier (27.8 Hz). That is the right slot for it, and the one [DimOS](entities/dimos.md) puts it in — a perception service an agent calls, not something in a servo loop.
- **Flagged the headline as an unfair comparison.** The site leads with "59 ms vs GPT-5.4 Mini 2.78 s (47×) and Gemini 2.5 Flash 3.79 s (64×)" — but those are **network APIs**, so the gap is dominated by round-trip and queueing. **The honest row is Qwen 3.5 4B + vLLM at 73 ms — same hardware, same batch, both local — a 21% edge, not 47×.** Everything is vendor-measured with no independent replication, and the AGX Orin row runs **Moondream 2, not 3.1**.
- **Pointing, arrived at twice independently.** Detect and Point put Moondream in the small club of VLMs emitting *spatial* output rather than text about space — the same signature capability as [Molmo](entities/molmo.md), whose pointing underpins [MolmoAct](entities/molmoact.md)'s action traces. Two unrelated small-VLM programs converged on it, and **neither is a VLA** — which suggests the capability is being selected for by the downstream robotics use case rather than by one lab's taste. Recorded on both pages.
- **Corrected my own draft before it shipped.** I had written an open question asking whether 3.1's weights were published at all, since the site's product blurb lists only "2B, 1B, and 0.5B checkpoints." **They are** — `moondream/moondream3.1-9B-A2B` is on Hugging Face. The staleness is in the **GitHub repo** (last pushed 2026-04-20, ~4 months) not the weights; the value moved into **Photon and Lens**, both commercial. Open weights ≠ open stack, and the latency ladder is a *Photon* result — but this is not a case of the open line quietly stopping.
- **Third instance of the same business model this week**: give away the model, sell what makes it fast. Alongside [Vulcan Robotics](entities/vulcan-robotics.md)'s rented inference and [Dimensional](entities/dimensional-inc.md)'s hosted teleop broker.
- Lint after: **953 pages · schema 0 · broken links 0 · wikilinks 0 · orphans 0 · index coverage 100% · dates 0 · source-count drift 0 · stale stubs 0.** Only the ~320 one-way citations remain, open by design.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | LangGraph — and a correction to this wiki's own inference
- **LangGraph had an entity page but no source page**, written secondhand from [DimOS](entities/dimos.md)'s usage — the same situation [Zenoh](entities/zenoh.md) was in. Now ingested: [LangGraph repo + docs](sources/langgraph.md), entity upgraded to primary.
- **The framework does ship the machinery.** Five pillars, two of which matter here: **durable execution** (*"persist through failures… automatically resuming from exactly where they left off"*) and **human-in-the-loop `interrupt()`** (*"inspecting and modifying agent state at any point during execution"*). MIT, 39,624★, cited production users Klarna/Replit/Elastic. It describes itself as **low-level infrastructure** and points quick-agent builders at Deep Agents instead.
- **Correction to my own inference.** The entity page had argued LangGraph's cycles and interrupts were *"the machinery for"* closed-loop replanning in DimOS, hedged with "whether or not DimOS uses it that way." The hedge was right and the emphasis was wrong. **A direct read of [`mcp_client.py`](https://github.com/dimensionalOS/dimos/blob/main/dimos/agents/mcp/mcp_client.py) shows DimOS uses the prebuilt ReAct tool-calling loop and nothing else**: `create_agent(model, tools, system_prompt)` with **no `checkpointer=`**, `.stream()` with **no `config`/`thread_id`**, and **zero** `MemorySaver`, `checkpointer`, `interrupt(`, or `thread_id` across every `.py` in `dimos/agents/`. No custom graph topology either — no `StateGraph(`, `add_node`, or `add_edge`.
- **So the [across-stacks synthesis](syntheses/agents/llm-agent-architecture-across-stacks.md)'s "most consequential gap" is not closed by DimOS** — how skill failures surface back to the planner for re-planning. That is now **verified for the largest stack in the comparison** rather than merely undocumented, and all four stacks still fail it.
- **The sharp version, and the reason it is worth recording: the fix is a dependency they already have.** DimOS is **one constructor argument** from durable execution and **one call** from human-in-the-loop. The capability is installed, imported, and unused. That reframes the wiki's standing critique of this category — the machinery for robust replanning is neither missing nor hard to obtain, it ships inside the framework the largest stack already depends on. **Nobody has needed it yet**, which is exactly what you would expect where **four stacks publish zero success rates between them**: durable execution pays off when runs are long and failures costly, and demos are neither.
- **Concrete consequence worth knowing before building on `McpClient`:** conversation history is a plain `list[BaseMessage]` on the module instance, so **the agent has no memory across a crash** — no resume, no replay, no inspection of prior state. For a robot meant to run a household task over minutes to hours that is a real limitation, and precisely what the durable-execution pillar exists for.
- Also noted: DimOS uses LangGraph's *highest*-level entry point (`create_agent`) rather than its low-level graph API, so **"DimOS chose a graph runtime" overstates the design intent** — what it chose was a maintained ReAct loop with MCP tool binding, which is the pragmatic call.
- New open question filed: **`interrupt()` as a robot safety rail.** The [guardrails synthesis](syntheses/agents/guardrails-for-robot-agents.md) argues the execution rail ships empty; human-in-the-loop interrupts are a ready-made mechanism and nothing in this wiki uses them. A design proposal rather than an ingest.
- Still uncovered: **LangChain itself**, despite being the substrate and supplying `create_agent`.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | LangChain + UME — and the lint's own gap check was defective
- Created [LangChain](sources/langchain.md) + [entity](entities/langchain.md), and [UME project page](sources/ume-project-page.md) + [entity](entities/ume.md).
- **Answering "why was LangChain uncovered": it was a defect in my lint, not a judgment.** LangChain appears in **12 pages** — above the threshold the §6 check uses — and was never flagged because **that check was a hand-written candidate list**. I picked `LangGraph` to grep for and omitted `LangChain`; the check found exactly what it was told to look for, and every term off the list was invisible. The substantive reason it stayed uncovered is defensible (no ingested robotics source treats LangChain as a *subject*) but it does not explain the miss.
- **Fixed properly: check #10 in [`scripts/lint_wiki.py`](../scripts/lint_wiki.py)** now extracts candidates automatically. Deliberately **high-precision, low-recall** — keeps only terms that look like proper nouns (internal capitals / hyphen / multi-word), skips anything whose slug is a substring of an existing page, filters licence strings and dates. **It would have caught LangChain.** It will not catch ordinary-looking single words (Drake, Zenoh, Moondream), which stay a human problem. Precision over recall on purpose: a noisy check gets ignored, which is how gaps persist.
- **It immediately surfaced three real ones**: **OpenAI (48 pages, no page)** — the most-cited organization in the wiki without one — **PyTorch (47)**, and **SIGReg (47)** from the LeJEPA line.
- **The LangChain finding worth keeping**: DimOS's robot agent calls `langchain.agents.create_agent` and `init_chat_model` **directly**, so the function the wiki's largest agentic-robotics stack actually invokes lives in **LangChain**, not [LangGraph](entities/langgraph.md) — which means my earlier "DimOS is a LangGraph stack" framing overstated the design intent twice over. Also flagged **framework churn** (chains → agents → "agent engineering platform," 2022–2026, orchestration now deferred to a package that did not exist at the start) as the real risk for a robot stack pinned to that surface.
- **[UME](entities/ume.md) is the first ingested source that *closes* the position-only recording gap rather than naming it.** Ant Group + Stanford (Cutkosky), arXiv 2606.14218. A **$1,900** upper-limb exoskeleton giving the operator **real-time haptic torque feedback** while **recording whole-arm configuration and joint torques**. Its thesis is the sentence this wiki has been circling all week: *"the majority of existing data collection pipelines still lack the ability to capture force and torque data for learning active compliant policies."* Legibility demo: **a blindfolded operator unsheathing a metal sword** on torque feedback alone.
- **The number to take away is the demonstration counts.** Occluded box pushing **26**, force-mediated flipping **40**, space-constrained GPU picking **42**, long-horizon mobile fridge retrieval **157** — **contact-rich, occluded, whole-body tasks at counts at or below the standard tabletop pick-and-place recipe**, against [X-VLA](entities/x-vla.md)'s 1,200 episodes / 50–60 operator-hours for cloth folding. If the **No-torque** ablation supports the causal reading, **force feedback is buying data efficiency, not just operator comfort** — which is exactly what the [XLeRobot bring-up plan](syntheses/projects/xlerobot-nav-manip-teleop-bringup.md)'s "~50 demos, 2–3 hours" budget is silently betting against.
- **It is the direct rebuttal to [UMI](entities/umi.md), and says so.** Same idea — *instrument the human, not the robot* — different bet: **UMI bets on visual context and portability, UME bets on torque.** Head-to-head on box pushing, GPU picking, and fridge retrieval, i.e. the occluded and force-mediated cases where a gripper-cam with no force channel should struggle. The acknowledgements thank Stanford's REAL Lab (Shuran Song), UMI's own lab.
- **But it publishes no success rates.** Videos labelled "Comparison with UMI" and "Comparison with No-torque" without outcomes. Per the [success-rate audit](syntheses/platforms/vla-success-rate-audit.md), **nothing here is a measurement yet** — the demo counts and the cost breakdown are the hard facts. Paper ingest filed as the highest-value follow-up.
- Cost places it usefully: **$1,900 exoskeleton + $9,533 robot ≈ $11.4 K**, above [XLeRobot](entities/xlerobot.md)/[Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md), ~half a [Stretch](entities/stretch.md). The **Hexfellow PCW-25 powered caster** is the line item to notice — *"improved compliance and precise odometry, while remaining fully holonomic"* — precisely the combination Sourccey surrendered with open-loop mecanum and XLeRobot's differential base cannot offer.
- Noted on [Sensori Robotics](entities/sensori-robotics.md): UME's platform uses an **"OpenArm 1.0 bimanual made by WowRobo,"** a second independent research platform sourcing "OpenArm" arms. Same lineage as Sensori's **OpenArm+**? Unestablished — but it hints at an open-hardware arm standard consolidating above the [SO-ARM101](entities/so-arm101.md) tier.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | UME paper — the ablations the project page withheld
- Created [UME paper](sources/ume-paper.md) (arXiv 2606.14218, Ant Group + Stanford, 12 Jun 2026); PDF into `raw/`. Upgraded [UME](entities/ume.md) from project-page-only to paper-backed. **This was the top backlog item and it delivered.**
- **The headline ablation, 20 trials per cell** — No-torque / UMI / UME: box pushing **0.50 / 0.40 / 0.90**, **box flipping 0.00 / 0.00 / 0.85**, GPU picking **0.75 / 0.00 / 0.95**, fridge retrieval **0.90 / 0.00 / 0.95**.
- **Applied the wiki's own [success-rate audit](syntheses/platforms/vla-success-rate-audit.md) rather than quoting the table.** Fisher exact at n=20, UME vs No-torque: box flipping **p<0.00001** and box pushing **p=0.014** separate; **GPU picking p=0.18 and fridge p=1.00 do not.** Versus UMI both tested gaps separate decisively. So the defensible claims are narrower than the table implies: **torque is load-bearing on force-mediated tasks, whole-arm configuration is load-bearing in constrained space, and on the long-horizon mobile task torque buys essentially nothing** (0.90 vs 0.95). The paper states that last one plainly rather than burying it.
- **Box flipping is the cleanest result in the paper and both baselines score exactly zero.** The mechanism is stated precisely: the task requires distinguishing **two visually identical states demanding opposite actions** — insufficient pressing force (keep pushing) vs sufficient force (begin the flip) — *"which can only be reliably distinguished through torque sensing."* **The strongest demonstration in this wiki that torque is information vision cannot supply**, not merely a control convenience. Anything trained on pixels-plus-position is blind to it by construction.
- **The fridge stage-wise table localizes UMI's failure to a single stage**: 0.95 at "open fridge" → **0.00 at "take out drink"**, from collisions while reaching around the door holder. End-effector pose plus IK cannot express the whole-arm posture clutter demands. **Scoped honestly on [UMI](entities/umi.md)**: this is a *re-parameterization ablation on UME's own data* against tasks chosen to stress what that representation lacks — strong evidence about **representation**, weaker about **the UMI system in the wild**, where portability is its actual argument.
- **Torque pays twice, and the payments are independent.** Beyond the policy gain, collection throughput is **3.3× demonstrations per minute** over torque-disabled UME, at **71% of unaided human speed**. For a data-collection budget that may be the larger number — it compounds over every hour of teleoperation.
- **Both open questions from the project-page ingest are now answered.** Torque **is** consumed as an observation channel (the No-torque baseline drops it "from the proprioception embedding"), and force feedback buys **both** policy quality and operator throughput, measured separately.
- **The recipe is the transferable part**: policies are **[ACT](entities/act.md)** — ResNet18, 4 encoder / 7 decoder layers, hidden 512, batch 200 — at **40 k steps on one RTX 4090 in 8 hours**, from **26–157 demonstrations**. Recorded on [ACT](entities/act.md) because it locates the bottleneck: with a torque channel, **plain ACT handles contact-rich whole-body behaviour the wiki usually attributes to larger policy classes. The gating resource was the sensor, not the architecture or the compute.**
- **86% of UME's $1,900 is actuators** — 8× Damiao DM-J4340 ($117) + 8× DM-J4310 ($88). The enabling choice is explicit: **quasi-direct-drive, low reduction**, against the **multi-stage planetary gearboxes** in the [Dynamixel](entities/dynamixel.md) actuators GELLO-class rigs use. Low reduction is what makes joints backdrivable enough for *transparent* feedback.
- **Propagated the measured cost to the platforms it bounds.** [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md)'s "positional only" and [XLeRobot](entities/xlerobot.md)'s lack of force sensing now carry the number: **it rules out a class of task** — the ones where the decision is invisible to a camera — rather than degrading everything uniformly. Sourccey's laundry folding is kinematically dominated and fine; pressing, sliding, and reaching into clutter are not.
- **Caveats recorded**: UME weighs **~12 kg** in PLA, wearable *"up to 2 hours"*, with fixed link lengths; **Franka teleoperation is simulation-only** pending hardware delivery, so universality is two real embodiments plus one simulated.
- **The missing comparison, now the top follow-up**: **UME vs UMI on collection throughput.** The 3.3× is against torque-disabled UME, not UMI — and a 12 kg exoskeleton against a handheld gripper is exactly the better-data-versus-easier-data trade a builder faces. Unmeasured.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | Niantic Spatial + LingBot-Map — a whole tradition was missing from this wiki
- Created [Niantic Spatial research page](sources/niantic-spatial-research.md), [LingBot-Map GitHub](sources/lingbot-map-github.md); entities [Niantic Spatial](entities/niantic-spatial.md), [LingBot-Map](entities/lingbot-map.md), [Robbyant](entities/robbyant.md); concept [visual relocalization and mapping](concepts/robotics/visual-relocalization-and-mapping.md).
- **The finding is a structural gap, not a new fact.** This wiki covers world models exhaustively — [generative-video](concepts/world-models/world-model-simulators.md), [JEPA](concepts/world-models/jepa.md), evaluation benchmarks — and had **no page for SLAM, visual relocalization, Gaussian splatting, or NeRF**. Those are a different tradition: **world models ask *what happens if I act*; this one asks *where am I, and what shape is this actual room*.** The gap was invisible even though the [XLeRobot bring-up plan](syntheses/projects/xlerobot-nav-manip-teleop-bringup.md)'s whole navigation leg is [RTAB-Map](entities/rtab-map.md) + [Nav2](entities/nav2.md) and [DimOS](entities/dimos.md) ships [GTSAM](entities/gtsam.md) pose-graph optimization. Cross-linked from [world model](concepts/world-models/world-model.md) so the two traditions point at each other.
- **Optimization → feed-forward, arrived at independently from AR and from robotics.** [Niantic](entities/niantic-spatial.md)'s own line runs **ACE** (CVPR 2023, *"relocalize in minutes"* by training a small per-scene network) → Scene Coordinate Reconstruction (ECCV 2024) → **feed-forward** localization and view synthesis by ICLR/CVPR 2026. [LingBot-Map](entities/lingbot-map.md) lands at the same place from robotics. **Per-instance optimization giving way to an amortized model once enough data exists** is a pattern this wiki keeps recording — [RoboTwin 2.0](entities/robotwin.md) replacing hand-written task programs with an MLLM, world models drifting from planning-in-a-model toward direct policies.
- **LingBot-Map moves drift correction from the optimizer into the architecture.** Classical SLAM handles long-horizon drift with loop closure plus back-end factor-graph optimization; LingBot-Map folds it into a transformer via **trajectory memory** and a **pose-reference window** — no loop-closure detector, no map to re-solve — and demonstrates a **25,000-frame, 13-minute walkthrough**. If that survives independent evaluation it is a genuine architectural shift. **16,471★ four months old, Apache-2.0, weights on HF and ModelScope.**
- **Paged KV cache attention in a reconstruction model** — LLM-serving infrastructure (vLLM's contribution) migrating into perception, treating **frames like tokens and the map like a KV cache**. Same pattern as [DimOS](entities/dimos.md) inheriting [LangGraph](entities/langgraph.md) for robot agents.
- **Held both vendors to the wiki's evidence standard.** LingBot-Map's **~20 FPS names no hardware** (`bf16`, `--compile`, FlashInfer all imply a workstation GPU), the README has **no accuracy table**, and **two KV-cache correctness bugs shipped and were fixed in three months**, both degrading pose and reconstruction quality at longer sequences — so 16.5k stars is **reach, not quality**. And Niantic lists **Robotics first among industries with no robotics deployment, benchmark, or customer shown**; the only concrete anchor is a city government, which is a GIS use case. Filed as **mapping infrastructure courting robotics**.
- **Recorded why [RTAB-Map](entities/rtab-map.md) stays the right choice for the XLeRobot plan** despite the feed-forward challengers: it is the only option with a **measured deployment on comparable hardware** ([Cutting the Cord](sources/cutting-the-cord-untethered-xlerobot.md), Orin Nano Super), and LingBot-Map publishes no edge latency and **does not describe relocalization against a prior map** — precisely what localization-only mode provides.
- **Assembled a program the wiki had been filing as four unrelated one-offs.** [Robbyant](entities/robbyant.md) is Ant Group's robotics arm, and this wiki had already met **LingBot-VLA** (policy, via a listicle), **LingBot-World** (world model, 5th of 10 on [WorldRoamBench](sources/worldroambench-paper.md), physics 47.32), **LingBot-Map** (reconstruction), and **[UME](entities/ume.md)** (teleoperation + data collection, with Stanford) — separately. That is **policy + world model + map + data-collection hardware, published openly, from one company**, a span matched here only by [NVIDIA GEAR](entities/nvidia-gear.md) and [Physical Intelligence](entities/physical-intelligence.md), neither of which ships an open exoskeleton.
- Noted Niantic's real asset: **a crowd-sourced *geometric* capture channel at city scale** (Scaniverse, ex-Pokémon-Go), which nothing else in this wiki has — the nearest analogues are [LeRobot](entities/lerobot.md) community datasets and [SmolVLA](entities/smolvla.md)'s 481 HF datasets, both *manipulation* data at hobbyist scale. Plus **SPZ**, an open splat format claiming **90% size reduction**, which only pays off if you expect to move a great deal of splat data.
- The Scaniverse URL given was a **login wall**; product detail came from `nianticspatial.com` and is flagged as vendor marketing rather than documentation.

## [2026-08-13] update | Scaniverse Capture page — content supplied, coverage firmed up
- The Scaniverse URL in this morning's ingest was a **login wall**; the user supplied the **Capture** page text directly. It **confirms** what was reconstructed from `nianticspatial.com` (SPZ at 90% reduction, iOS/Android + 360° cameras + drones, on-device splats and meshes) and adds the rest of the capability list, the paid/enterprise tiering, and the Greenwich Power Station showcase. Provenance note on the [source](sources/niantic-spatial-research.md) updated from "blocked" to "vendor marketing copy, confirmed" — still **no specifications, accuracy figures, or benchmarks anywhere on it**.
- **The new analytical point is a pairing that only shows up when the full capability list is visible.** Two of Scaniverse's five stated capabilities sit adjacent: ***"no expensive equipment or specialized training required"*** and ***"built for Niantic Spatial pipelines — connect directly with Niantic Spatial's reconstruction and localization workflows."*** **Deskill the capture as far as it will go, then couple the output to your own stack.** SPZ's 90% size reduction is the enabling piece — phone-captured splats are only shippable at volume if they are small — and volume is the entire point, since it all feeds the **Large Geospatial Model**. That is the flywheel stated in product terms rather than inferred.
- **The robotics-relevant version of the claim is real, and still not a deployment**: a fleet operator could map a facility with **phones and drones instead of survey equipment or a dedicated mapping robot**. A genuine cost argument. [Niantic Spatial](entities/niantic-spatial.md) still shows no robot running on any of it.

## [2026-08-13] update | Niantic publications back-catalogue — two findings well beyond Niantic
- The `#publications` anchor carries the full list back to **2017**, which changes the picture of the group substantially and surfaces one paper that belongs to a different wiki thread entirely.
- **This group invented self-supervised monocular depth.** **monodepth** (Godard, Mac Aodha, Brostow, CVPR 2017) and **monodepth2** (ICCV 2019) are theirs, along with Depth Hints, Learning Stereo from Single Images, Footprints and Free Space, and The Temporal Opportunist. So the arc is **three eras** — self-supervised monocular depth (2017–2021) → visual relocalization / scene-coordinate regression (2021–2025) → feed-forward reconstruction and splatting (2025–2026) — each enabling the next: cheap depth from ordinary cameras made map-free relocalization plausible, which made amortized reconstruction the obvious step. Recorded on [Niantic Spatial](entities/niantic-spatial.md); **monodepth2 has no page here** despite being the ancestor of a lot of cheap-robot depth perception.
- **The finding that travels furthest is a 2021 methodology paper.** *"On the Limits of Pseudo Ground Truth in Visual Camera Re-Localisation"* (Brachmann, Humenberger, Rother, Sattler, ICCV 2021) argues relocalization benchmarks score against **ground truth produced by an algorithm**, so leaderboards partly measure agreement with whichever reference method generated the labels.
- **That makes four independent instances of "the instrument is not measuring what the field thinks it measures"**, and they decompose cleanly by *what* is broken: [LIBERO-PRO](sources/libero-pro-paper.md) — the **tasks** (evaluation set is the training set); [VP²](sources/vp2-paper.md) — the **metrics** (perceptual scores mis-rank for control, sign-dependent); the [success-rate audit](syntheses/platforms/vla-success-rate-audit.md) — the **sample sizes**; and this — the **labels**. Four subfields, ~5 years apart, **and they do not cite each other**. Added as an addendum to the audit, which is where the thread lives.
- Also noted: **ICRA 2021's "Learning to Predict Repeatability of Interest Points" is the only robotics-venue paper in the entire list** — which sharpens this morning's read that Niantic Spatial is mapping infrastructure courting robotics rather than a robotics company. The publication record says the same thing the product pages do.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | Pseudo Ground Truth + Niantic/Flexion/NVIDIA sim2real + Flexion
- Created [Pseudo Ground Truth paper](sources/pseudo-ground-truth-paper.md) (PDF into `raw/`), [Niantic/Flexion/NVIDIA sim2real](sources/niantic-flexion-nvidia-sim2real.md), and [Flexion Robotics](entities/flexion.md).
- **The measurement thread's fourth instance is now read in full, and it is the sharpest of the four.** Re-localisation benchmarks generate poses with a **reference algorithm**, so they *"measure how well each method replicates the results of the reference algorithm."* Swapping depth-SLAM pGT for SfM pGT on 7Scenes moves **Active Search from last to first — +29.8 points absolute** (on Pumpkin and Red Kitchen, from <50% to >99%) while the depth-based leaders fall to the bottom, each beaten by its own RGB-only counterpart.
- **The mechanism is bias, not noise**: *"methods that optimise a similar cost function as the reference algorithm better replicate the local minima and **imperfections** of the pGT."* A method is scored on **agreeing with one algorithm's errors**, not on being right. The authors call it *"fundamental"* and say they *"do not see a solution."*
- **Two beliefs it deflates**: that scene-coordinate regression beats classical feature matching, and that RGB-D beats RGB. **Brachmann is the scene-coordinate-regression line** (DSAC, ACE) and the paper's first casualty is his own camp's claim — worth noting, since the wiki's other measurement papers mostly critique someone else's results.
- **It contributes two things back to the thread.** Its remedy *"task-specific evaluation… in the context of AR, robotic navigation"* is the same move [VP²](sources/vp2-paper.md) makes independently two years later. And preferring the method *"not best under any pGT but good under all"* is the same **robustness-over-peak** conclusion [RoboTwin 2.0](entities/robotwin.md) reaches for VLA pretraining. The four instances now decompose cleanly by what is broken: **labels** (this) / **metrics** (VP²) / **tasks** (LIBERO-PRO) / **sample sizes** (the audit) — four subfields, five years, no cross-citation.
- **Filed the open transposition** on the audit: LIBERO's ground truth is simulator task success, but **[RoboTwin 2.0](entities/robotwin.md)'s expert demonstrations *are* generated by an MLLM-plus-planner pipeline, and policies are scored on tasks that pipeline could solve.** Nobody has asked whether synthetic-demonstration benchmarks have a pseudo-ground-truth problem.
- **The robotics evidence I flagged as missing this morning arrived, and it is strong.** [Niantic + Flexion + NVIDIA](sources/niantic-flexion-nvidia-sim2real.md): scan an office with an **off-the-shelf 360° camera** → Gaussian-splat twin **plus a collision mesh derived from the same reconstruction** → **USDZ/NuRec into [Isaac Lab](entities/nvidia-isaac-lab.md)** → RGB-only navigation policy by massively parallel RL **on one GPU** → **zero-shot transfer to a real humanoid**, robust to rearranged furniture.
- **The load-bearing design choice is one reconstruction yielding both layers**, so appearance and collision *"stay aligned by construction… no separate cross-registration step."* The failure it removes is worth remembering generally: *"a small mismatch between a rendered wall and a collision wall can teach a humanoid to slide through obstacles it can see."*
- **The benchmark is better controlled than most this wiki ingests, and it is on a company blog**: four policies × two scenes, **1,024 rollouts each with spawn and target poses reused across conditions** so the comparison isolates sensor modality × training environment. RGB-in-3DGS **97.8 vs 93.8** (easy) and **75.0 vs 70.9** (hard) against the depth baseline — I computed both: **p<0.0001 and p=0.037, both separate.** It is *"the only RGB setup that matches or exceeds the conventional depth baseline."*
- **It narrows rather than overturns the morning's read.** Niantic supplies the *world*, [Flexion](entities/flexion.md) the *policy and deployment*, NVIDIA the *simulator* — still mapping infrastructure for robotics, now with a named customer instead of none.
- **Two things worth carrying**: the policy is **deliberately site-specific** (*"specialization unlocks capability"*), which is the opposite of the generalist bet [X-VLA](entities/x-vla.md)/[π0](entities/pi-zero.md)/[GR00T](entities/nvidia-groot.md) make — and **Gaussian splats are used as an RL renderer rather than a viewer**, a different requirement where throughput and viewpoint generalisation matter more than single-view fidelity. Recorded on [visual relocalization and mapping](concepts/robotics/visual-relocalization-and-mapping.md).
- **[Flexion](entities/flexion.md)** filed: Zürich, sells the *autonomy stack* not robots, hardware-agnostic by intent, betting on **simulation + RL with minimal human involvement** — a **data-light bet against the demonstration-collection economics** the rest of this wiki tracks. Flagged the tension honestly: *"across any hardware and task"* is marketing; the evidence is **navigation only** (the easier sim2real case) with a **site-specific** policy. Its **Reflect v0/v1.0** posts carry the long-horizon and generality claims and are un-ingested.

## [2026-08-13] ingest | Flexion Reflect v0 + v1.0 — two long-standing findings closed
- Created [Reflect v0](sources/flexion-reflect-v0.md) (architecture, Nov 2025) and [Reflect v1.0](sources/flexion-reflect-v1.md) (results, Jun 2026). **The most consequential agentic ingest in this wiki.**
- **The [across-stacks synthesis](syntheses/agents/llm-agent-architecture-across-stacks.md) now has five stacks, and Reflect supersedes its headline finding twice over.**
- **Finding 1 closed — the VLA/agentic bifurcation.** The synthesis had held since May that *"the LLM-agent path and the VLA path do not coexist in any of these stacks"*; [DimOS](entities/dimos.md) narrowed it (LeRobot export, no VLA in the control path). **Reflect v1.0's motion layer is *"a VLA trained on real-world data + RL-based skills"* under a custom VLM mission controller.** A VLA now runs inside an agentic control path. **But not a simple reversal**: Flexion is candid that the VLA is the stack's *weakest* link — *"achieving high reliability in such settings is difficult, especially for a **free-moving humanoid rather than a fixed-base manipulation system**. We are already working on the next logical solution: solving these tasks with RL."* The **direction of travel is away from the VLA**, the inverse of the field's prevailing bet.
- **Finding 2 closed — the closed-loop-replanning gap**, which that synthesis called *"the most consequential gap between published demo behavior and robust deployment"* and which four stacks left undocumented (DimOS had [LangGraph](entities/langgraph.md)'s interrupts installed and **unused**). Flexion names the failure mode exactly: off-the-shelf VLMs *"act **too eagerly**. Instead of **visually verifying that the previous tool call has completed and that the scene satisfies the preconditions for the next step**, they emit the next logically plausible tool call too quickly."* And fixes it — not by prompting, not with a better base model, not with SFT: **on a 16-step mission, end-to-end completion goes base-VLM ≈ 0 → SFT 38% → SFT+RL 90%.** SFT alone *"does not hold up when the robot must make decisions amid ambiguity, recover from failed attempts, and keep progressing when the plan diverges."*
- **The first published success rate from any stack on that page** — and I recorded the caveat with it: **n is unstated**, the evaluation set undescribed, and everything else in the post is video. "Get the trial count" is now the open item.
- **Their compounding argument is this wiki's success-rate logic applied to system composition**, and better stated than the wiki had it: *"A navigation policy that works 95% of the time, a grasp that works 90% of the time, and a planner that occasionally misreads the scene do not combine into a reliable system. **They compound into failure.**"*
- **It also inverts convergence 3's implication.** Skills still live below the LLM — but Flexion's are **RL-trained whole-body policies and a VLA**, not YOLO/AprilTag/Nav2. The pattern survives; the reading that it means *"classical robotics with an LLM dispatcher bolted on top"* does not.
- **v0's design statement is the best one-line summary of this pattern the wiki has**: *"language drives intent and physics enforces feasibility."* Recorded on [LLM-agent architecture](concepts/agents/llm-agent-architecture.md).
- **The data thesis partly survives its own test.** v0 bet that *"training robots with data collected manually for every possible scenario is a dead end"* and that sim+RL would carry the stack. v1.0 delivers for **locomotion, navigation, and contact-rich box handling** (100 g–3.5 kg with one policy; 100+ consecutive stair traversals) — and **binds exactly where this wiki's other sources say it binds: dexterous manipulation**, the one layer trained on teleoperated data and the one they call unreliable. **A live, testable disagreement with [UME](entities/ume.md)**, whose answer to the same bottleneck was to make demonstrations *better* by adding torque rather than to remove them.
- **Fifth instance of on-robot control + off-board reasoning**: Jetson Orin in a backpack, VLM in the cloud, **Thor named as the path to self-contained autonomy** — joining [XLeRobot](entities/xlerobot.md), [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md), [DimOS](entities/dimos.md), and the Spark serving estimate. Nobody runs frontier reasoning on the robot; everyone's roadmap says they will.
- Also picked up from the site: **[Flexion](entities/flexion.md) raised $50 M** (Nov 2025), **Flexion Robotics AG**, Zürich + San Francisco. The funding post is un-ingested.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | GCS — Motion Planning around Obstacles with Convex Optimization
- Created [GCS motion-planning paper](sources/gcs-motion-planning-paper.md) (PDF into `raw/`), the concept page [Graphs of convex sets](concepts/robotics/graphs-of-convex-sets.md), and [Tobia Marcucci](entities/tobia-marcucci.md). Marcucci, Petersen, von Wrangel & [Tedrake](entities/russ-tedrake.md); arXiv 2205.04422 (2022-05-09), **Science Robotics 8(84), 2023-11-15**.
- **This ingest corrects the wiki, not just extends it.** [Motion planning](concepts/robotics/motion-planning.md) was built from [Bekris et al. 2024](sources/state-of-robot-motion-generation-2024.md), which files the optimization-based family as *"fast, high-quality when they work; **local minima on non-convex problems**"* — and names GCS in the same clause. **That is exactly wrong about GCS**, which solves a single convex program plus cheap rounding and returns the *global* optimum. Both pages now carry the correction: the right qualifier is **"restricted problem class,"** not "local minima." The survey compressed a method published a year before it into the property it was built to eliminate.
- **The mechanism, in one line**: decompose free space into overlapping convex safe regions → vertices; overlap → edges; pair each vertex with a **Bézier trajectory segment + a Bézier time-scaling function** → the planning problem *is* a shortest path in a graph of convex sets. The convex-hull property converts `r(s) ∈ Qᵢ ∀s` (infinitely many constraints) into `d+1` constraints on control points.
- **The structural win is the binary count, and the maze makes it undeniable.** Prior MICP planners cut one trajectory into segments and use a binary per (segment, region) — worst case `|I|²`. On a 2,500-cell maze that is **6.25 million binaries**, *"well beyond the capability of today's solvers."* GCS uses two binaries per intersecting pair: **5,198**. And on that maze the relaxation returned **binary `ϕₑ` outright** — no rounding, `δ_opt = 0%`, certified.
- **The result I most want to carry beyond planning: it certifies itself, per query, for free.** `C_relax ≤ C_opt ≤ C_round`, so `δ_relax := (C_round − C_relax)/C_relax` upper-bounds the true gap and costs one subtraction. **Almost nothing else in this wiki's corpus does this.** The structural contrast is with [robot policy evaluation](concepts/robotics/robot-policy-evaluation.md), where a policy's quality is knowable only by rollouts and ±2 pp takes ≈1,030 of them. Here the answer arrives with its own error bar *before the robot moves*.
- **It beats PRM on both axes on all five 7-DoF KUKA iiwa tasks** — including shortcut-PRM, the version practitioners actually deploy. Lengths 3.3/2.1/2.6/3.5/1.8 rad vs PRM 9.7/6.7/3.7/6.1/4.2 and shortcut 5.6/5.2/2.7/3.8/1.9; runtimes 0.11/0.01/0.05/0.14/0.08 s vs 0.20–0.26 and 0.44–0.61. **8 IRIS regions replaced a 15,000-sample roadmap.** Quadrotor ×100 random buildings: **95% within 1% of optimal, worst case 2.9%**. Dual-arm **14-DoF** with self-collision: 4.0/8.4/12.9 s, two provably optimal.
- **The reframing that matters more than the benchmark**: the authors call GCS a **generalization of PRM** — *"each collision-free sample is expanded to a collision-free convex region, that is inflated as much as the obstacles allow."* So the optimization-based family **absorbs** the roadmap idea rather than competing with it. Recorded on both [motion planning](concepts/robotics/motion-planning.md) and the concept page.
- **Recorded the limits at the same weight as the results, because the authors do.** No dynamics (derivative-coupling constraints are nonconvex under the joint shape/timing parameterization *"even for a linear control system"*), no task-space constraints, no contact; higher derivatives are *regularized*, not bounded; the control-point condition is sufficient-only; the SPP in GCS is NP-hard and the rounding is a heuristic. Plus MOSEK needed a primal-form flag to avoid numerical trouble, at real cost (quadrotor relaxation: median 3.7 s, **max 31.2 s**).
- **The hidden human labor is the finding a lint pass would miss.** The convex decomposition is an **input**, not an output — and the IRIS **seed poses were placed by hand via inverse kinematics**, with graph connectivity checked visually. *"Automatic seeding of the regions is certainly possible, but we have found that producing seeds manually … is straightforward and highly effective."* Region build 53 s vs PRM's 16-min roadmap looks favorable until you notice **only one of the two needed a person**. This is GCS's equivalent of reward shaping in RL or demo collection in [imitation learning](concepts/learning/imitation-learning.md): real, offline, and absent from the runtime table. Also: the fast IRIS implementation **does not certify its regions collision-free** — so the end-to-end guarantee is optimality-within-the-regions, not safety-of-the-regions.
- **[Drake](entities/drake.md) gets its abstract design thesis cashed out.** Its "unified LP/QP/SOCP/SDP/SOS/MIP interface" reads like plumbing until you see that the SPP-in-GCS solver and `IrisInConfigurationSpace` ship there and *are* this planner. The paper's central tradeoff — **Bézier control points instead of SOS polynomials, so the program is an SOCP rather than a mixed-integer SDP** — is a choice made inside Drake's own solver taxonomy, and it is what lets the planner reach the `C⁴` differentiability quadrotor differential flatness requires.
- **Sharpened the Tedrake page's bridge claim with a date.** The same senior author says *"ML has outrun theory"* and roboticists are becoming behavioral scientists — and published a planner that **proves** its answer optimal. The defensible reading is not "learning replaced model-based control" but **"use the guarantee wherever the problem admits one, and learn the rest"** — and this paper is unusually explicit about where the guarantee stops.
- Split the credit deliberately: **the mathematical object is [Marcucci](entities/tobia-marcucci.md)'s, the planner is Tedrake's group's.** The planning paper treats shortest-paths-in-GCS as an external *"modeling language."* That framework paper is **not in `raw/`** and is now the most obvious follow-up ingest.
- Glossary picked up **GCS, IRIS, MICP, PRM, SOCP, SOS** — the wiki had *no* convex-optimization or classical-planning vocabulary at all, which is itself a coverage tell.
- Open and unresolved: **did GCS reach deployment?** Drake ships it; Tedrake now runs [Walden Robotics](entities/walden-robotics.md) on [LBMs](concepts/learning/large-behavior-models.md) + [Diffusion Policy](entities/diffusion-policy.md). Whether GCS sits in an industrial stack or remained the elegant alternative the learned line routed around is the more interesting question, and nothing here answers it.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | GCS deployment — Tedrake's 2024 seminar + the ARM/Dexai record
- **Query: "did GCS reach deployment?"** — the open question filed with this morning's [GCS paper ingest](sources/gcs-motion-planning-paper.md). **Answer: narrowly yes**, and the evidence is a direct quote.
- Created [Planning with Graphs of Convex Sets (in the age of foundation models)](sources/tedrake-gcs-foundation-models-talk.md) (MIT Robotics Seminar, [Tedrake](entities/russ-tedrake.md), 2024-04-07, 62 min, ingested via auto-captions), [Time-Optimal Motion Planning Using Convex Sets (ARM Institute)](sources/arm-institute-gcs-dexai-project.md), and [Dexai Robotics](entities/dexai-robotics.md).
- **The finding, verbatim (34:13)**: *"Dexai is a local company… They're doing food preparation… so they switched from their pretty optimized — this is like **time is money** for these guys — **PRM-based planner. Now they're using GCS in production**."* Corroborated independently by [ARM](sources/arm-institute-gcs-dexai-project.md): commercial transition, *"multiple customer sites across the nation,"* **>$10,000/robot/year**, five delivered modules including **`WarmGcsPlanner`** — the productization work was **repeated-query latency**, not the optimality machinery.
- **The qualifier is the content, and I recorded it at equal weight**: *"in a setting where you're going to be making plans all day long, you're willing to precompute once, it makes a lot of sense."* Every restriction the paper lists is **free** in that regime — fixed workcell amortizes the decomposition, multi-query pays, free-space transit needs no dynamics/contact/task-space constraints, and cycle time is already the customer's metric. Tedrake gives the negative case himself: for a mobile manipulator not chasing time-optimality the answer *"change[s] completely"* — fast approximate covers straight from perception. **So: a strong result in a narrow market, not general infrastructure**, and the wiki now says so on every page that carries the claim.
- **What GCS displaced matters as much as that it shipped.** Not a greenfield deployment — a *"pretty optimized"* PRM, in production, on the axis PRM was supposed to own (multi-query). That is the paper's iiwa benchmark reproduced by a customer with money on it.
- **The paper's biggest practical hole is closed.** Hand-placed IRIS seeds → **visibility graph + approximate minimum clique cover**. The load-bearing idea deserves its own line: *mutual straight-line visibility is a combinatorial proxy for convexity*, so "where do the convex sets go?" becomes a graph problem with known approximations. Described as *"almost turnkey."* The residual labor **moved rather than vanished** — the judgment is now coverage policy, and Tedrake's own guidance is that covering every corner of a high-dimensional C-space is *"a false goal."*
- **The other stated limits are under attack, with results**: geodesic convexity for manifolds (SO(2) bases, continuous-rotation joints); **analytical IK to build regions on a constraint manifold** for bimanual task-space constraints; and contact via **SDP relaxation of the contact QCQP, making each mode's spectrahedron a GCS vertex**. The bar-lowering line is the one to keep: the relaxation *"doesn't have to be perfect… it just has to be strong enough to help you know which path to take through the graph."*
- **A correction to a decade of contact-graph papers, stated in passing**: the combinatorial hardness of contact is **not** making/breaking contact (that can be smoothed — cf. randomized smoothing) but **which side you contact on**.
- **The segment I did not expect: the GCS dual is a value function.** Shortest-path LP duals are cost-to-go; the GCS dual is a **piecewise-affine lower bound on the value function over every set**, upgradeable to quadratic/polynomial (⇒ SOS) for tighter bounds — one offline solve covering all initial conditions, i.e. **a policy, not a plan**. Push it back to the primal and it looks like propagating **probability distributions** through the graph, higher-order polynomials ↔ higher moments ⇒ planning under uncertainty. Filed on [optimal control](concepts/robotics/optimal-control.md); this is what makes the "GCS as robotics' missing MCTS" pitch technical rather than rhetorical.
- **TAMP picked up a genuinely new idea**: a box-shuffling instance whose right combinatorial object was a **walk on the permutohedron**, not a shortest path, because box order is irrelevant. Generalized heuristic, stated twice: *shrink the sets to points, ask which network-flow problem you are left with* — strong convex flow ⇒ strong GCS extension; NP-hard flow (TSP) ⇒ GCS won't save you.
- **Symmetry-kills-the-relaxation, observed in the field**: a UAV routed the long way around rather than through **one of two near-identical windows**, because the relaxation split probability between them. That is footnote 3 of the paper happening on hardware. Plus a real design dial: smaller regions tighten the convex approximation of nonlinear dynamics but grow the discrete problem — *"you can move the work from the convex optimization into the discrete problem and vice versa."*
- **The honest limit, from the author, unprompted (61:19)**: *"I don't think we can solve dexterous hands with GCS as it is. The graph gets too big. I need help."* His fix — roll out a generalist policy a few times to decide which nodes to expand — is **a plan, not a result**, and nothing in this wiki demonstrates it.
- **The talk also sharpens the [Tedrake](entities/russ-tedrake.md) bridge into a thesis rather than a juxtaposition**: AlphaGo went behavior-cloning → search, robotics has step one ([Diffusion Policy](entities/diffusion-policy.md)/[LBMs](concepts/learning/large-behavior-models.md)) and is **missing its MCTS**; planning strengthens a learned policy *"just by virtue of looking ahead a few steps"* with **no additional learning**; and his objection to RL is optimizer strength, not tribe — *"minimum distance… plus maybe minimum energy. **No tuning.**"* The loop already runs one way: **GCS-planned contact trajectories were loaded into a diffusion policy as co-training curriculum.**
- Updated [GCS](concepts/robotics/graphs-of-convex-sets.md) (deployment, seeding, extensions, looseness), [GCS paper](sources/gcs-motion-planning-paper.md) (three open questions struck through and answered), [motion planning](concepts/robotics/motion-planning.md), [TAMP](concepts/robotics/task-and-motion-planning.md), [optimal control](concepts/robotics/optimal-control.md), [Tedrake](entities/russ-tedrake.md), [Drake](entities/drake.md) (*"pretty mature"* implementation → a shipping product; the strongest deployment evidence for any Drake component here), [Marcucci](entities/tobia-marcucci.md), glossary (**QCQP**, **spectrahedron**, **visibility graph**), index.
- **Caveats recorded on the pages, not just here**: auto-caption ingest, so **student and collaborator surnames are deliberately not asserted**; the deployment claim is a **spoken remark from April 2024** with nothing tracked since; the ARM ROI figure is **vendor-side with no methodology**; and the extensions are described from slides, with **none of the follow-on papers ingested**.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | SPP in Graphs of Convex Sets (framework paper) + the constrained-QP safety layer
- Two threads, one session: the **framework paper under GCS**, and a targeted dig into **what actually keeps learned-policy rollouts safe on MIT/TRI hardware**.

### 1. [Shortest Paths in Graphs of Convex Sets](sources/shortest-paths-in-graphs-of-convex-sets-paper.md) — Marcucci, Umenberger, **Parrilo** & [Tedrake](entities/russ-tedrake.md); arXiv 2101.11565 → **SIAM J. Optimization 34(1):507–532, 2024**. PDF into `raw/`.
- **The wiki's inferred credit split is confirmed, and the co-author list is the evidence.** [Marcucci](entities/tobia-marcucci.md) with **Parrilo** — the SOS/semidefinite-relaxation authority — in a SIAM optimization journal, Tedrake fourth. This is an optimization paper that happens to have a roboticist on it; the planner is the robotics paper.
- **The mechanism, finally on the page it belongs on.** Extend the classical **network-flow LP**, not a new encoding. The cost wants to be `ℓ_e(x_u,x_v)·y_e`, which is *undefined* exactly when it matters (`ℓ_e = ∞`, `y_e = 0` — an infinite edge length is how dynamics and continuity get encoded). **Perspective operators** fix it: `ℓ̃_e` equals the product when flow is positive and **evaluates to zero when flow is zero even if the length is infinite**. What's left is bilinear, and bilinears get relaxed by multiplying valid flow inequalities against `x_v ∈ X_v`.
- **Two properties worth carrying beyond GCS.** The relaxation is **set-based** — it never touches the inequalities defining `X_v`, so the sets may be **black boxes behind a separation oracle**. And it is **exact at extreme points of the flow polytope**, which — since a genuine path *is* an extreme point — is the entire correctness proof, in one lemma, generalizing an RLT result from polytopes to arbitrary closed convex sets.
- **Placed in the relaxation family, by the authors**: first-level **RLT** specialized to this bilinear structure; collapses to a **McCormick envelope** on real intervals; *"as tight as the first level of the Lovász–Schrijver hierarchy… without semidefinite constraints"*; and provably **not** the convex hull in general — that would solve an NP-hard bilinear program in polynomial time.
- **NP-hardness is a two-paragraph gem.** `X_s={0}`, `X_t={1}`, `X_v=[0,1]`, *squared* Euclidean length: a `K`-edge path spaces uniformly for cost `1/K`, so minimizing cost = **maximizing vertices visited** = Hamiltonian Path. One-dimensional intervals suffice. Thm 3.2 closes every escape: acyclic **and** disjoint **and** positively homogeneous — still NP-hard.
- **A redundancy that stops being redundant**, and a good general reflex: the **degree constraint** is provably redundant in the shortest-path LP and load-bearing in the MICP — without it a five-vertex example returns a *disjoint cycle* at below-optimal cost.
- **The target application is not motion planning — it is hybrid/PWA optimal control**, and the modeling choice is the contribution: *"we do not use binary variables to encode the discrete mode… but, instead, we use them to select the **transitions** between modes."* Quadratic in mode count instead of linear, and on a PWA double integrator (`T=30`, `|V|=212`, `|E|=1435`, sets in `ℝ⁶`): prior state of the art **93% relaxation gap / 1011 s ≈ 17 min**; this **20% / 7.1 s**. ~142×. Filed on [optimal control](concepts/robotics/optimal-control.md), which now has a hybrid-MPC section.
- **The qualitative half of that result is better than the ratio.** The baseline's relaxed trajectory *"heads straight to the goal"* with uninformative mode indicators; this one **avoids the low-controllability regions and clusters along the true optimum** — *"our relaxation contains detailed information about the optimal path."* A relaxation you can read beats a bound you can only compare.
- **Corrects a framing the wiki inherited from the planner.** GCS does not *lack* dynamics: the [planner](sources/gcs-motion-planning-paper.md) drops them because derivative coupling is nonconvex **under its Bézier shape/timing parameterization**. In state-space form with one region per mode, discrete-time linear dynamics are a perfectly ordinary convex edge constraint. Two papers, opposite sides of the [optimal control](concepts/robotics/optimal-control.md) page's central tradeoff.
- **Symmetry now has three independent sightings** and is the framework's characteristic weakness: the planner's footnote-3 argument for randomized rounding, this paper's own §9.4 counterexample (*"arbitrarily loose"* — 100% gap in a variant), and the UAV-and-two-windows anecdote from the [seminar](sources/tedrake-gcs-foundation-models-talk.md).
- **And the loosening has a counterintuitive shape**: worst-case gap goes 2.1% (nominal) → 9.1% (bigger sets) → 28.9% (n: 4→20) → 32.9% (edges 100→500) → **back to 5.3%** at 250 vertices with the *same* 500 edges. **Density, not size** — cycles are the mechanism. Against McCormick: median gap 29–34%, 10–13× slower.
- **Appendix B is the plan-to-policy result, three years early.** The dual assigns each vertex an affine potential `r_v^⊤x_v + p_v` and maximizes the source-to-target jump — i.e. the *"piecewise-affine lower bound on the value function"* Tedrake pitched in 2024 as GCS's route to being robotics' missing MCTS was already written into the 2021 framework paper.
- Appendix A offers the same technique for **TSP-, MST-, and other graph-problems-with-neighborhoods** in high dimensions, unevaluated — the widest-open door in the paper.
- Glossary picked up **perspective function/operator, McCormick envelope, RLT, PWA**.

### 2. New concept: [operational space control](concepts/robotics/operational-space-control.md) — the constrained-QP layer under learned policies
- **Question asked: what enforces constraints during policy rollouts in Tedrake's lab? Answer: Appendix D.1 of the [Diffusion Policy paper](sources/diffusion-policy-paper.md)**, which the wiki had ingested without recording its control stack.
- **The policy never commands the robot.** It emits desired end-effector poses into a **mid-level controller**: a **differential-kinematics QP** whose solution is Euler-integrated to joint positions and tracked by a joint-level controller. *"This formulation allows us to impose constraints such as **collision avoidance for the two arms and the table, safety region for end effector and joint limits**… **particularly valuable for safeguarding the learned policy during hardware deployment**."* Plus null-space regulation of the redundant DoF.
- **A second controller, and this one is textbook OSC**: for haptic teleop, a pure-torque QP *"formulated using **Operational Space Control** (Khatib 1987)… operating at **200 Hz**, where position, velocity, and torque limits are added as constraints, and the primary spatial objective and secondary null-space posture objectives are posed as **costs**."* Needs a good Franka model *"including reflected rotor inertias."* **Collision avoidance is not enabled in this mode** — inference uses the other one.
- **The design principle is the finding**: *objectives are costs, limits are hard constraints.* A QP either satisfies its constraints or is infeasible, so an unsafe request becomes **the closest safe motion**, not a clipped unsafe one. Tedrake's textbook states the same thing about diff IK: solving inside the feasible set *"can be much better than… simply trimming any velocities to respect the constraints after the fact."*
- **The rate gap reframed.** Policies run at **10 Hz**, the mid-level controller *"runs around 1kHz"* and interpolates between policy ticks — the constraint set is re-solved **~100× per policy decision**. The [control-rate ladder](syntheses/platforms/control-rate-ladder.md) reads that ratio as *the policy is too slow*; from here it is **what makes a slow stochastic policy deployable at all**. Both entries added to the ladder, with a callout.
- **Same architecture at program scale**: the [TRI LBM](sources/tri-lbm-paper.md) stack is a custom **joint impedance controller** with a **differential IK controller on top** that *"also handles collision avoidance,"* policies at 10 Hz. Recorded on that page (fetched from the arXiv HTML; its "full PDF not ingested" caveat still stands for everything else).
- **Recorded what it is not, at equal weight.** Model-based and geometric — the hazards you can write down from a URDF. **No** perception-driven avoidance of unmodelled obstacles, **no** CBF or reachability certificate, **no** protection against a semantically wrong action inside the feasible set, and nothing a standards body would accept. It is the *uncertified* version of the "certified classical safety layer wrapping an uncertified learned policy" pattern that [robot safety standards](concepts/robotics/robot-safety-standards.md) predicted — now noted there, and next to [Halos](entities/nvidia-halos.md), which supplies the conformity evidence this lacks.
- **It also names a hole in the [control-abstraction taxonomy](concepts/robotics/control-abstraction-levels.md)**: "level 3 — high-level commands to a pretrained policy" is only safe because a constrained controller bounds it. The taxonomy scores how *high* the model sits, never how *tightly the bottom is bounded* — independent axes, and a level-1 model behind a hard envelope may be safer than a level-3 model without one.
- Filed the relation to [whole-body control](concepts/robotics/whole-body-control.md): classical WBC ≈ OSC + null-space prioritization on a floating base, and the learned WBC policies this wiki tracks **give up the hard-constraint property** — which is why manipulation stacks still keep a QP between policy and hardware. Glossary: **OSC**, **Differential IK**.
- Open and worth chasing: *Safe, Task-Consistent Manipulation with Operational Space Control Barrier Functions* (IROS 2025) — the CBF upgrade that would give this envelope an actual guarantee.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | OSCBF — the safety layer with a proof, and the experiment nobody has run
- Created [OSCBF paper](sources/oscbf-paper.md) (Morton & Pavone, Stanford; arXiv 2503.06736, **IROS 2025**; PDF into `raw/`) and [Marco Pavone](entities/marco-pavone.md). Requested directly off the backlog item filed the same day.
- **It is the formal upgrade to the envelope the [operational space control](concepts/robotics/operational-space-control.md) page had just documented — and it is aimed at us.** Its reference **[1] is [Diffusion Policy](sources/diffusion-policy-paper.md)**, cited for the claim that learning-based controllers *"may be quite versatile, but do not provide guarantees on safety."* [2] and [3] are DROID and OXE, cited for the point that teleoperated demonstration data is collected as **end-effector** motion — which is *why* the operational-space layer is the right place to intervene.
- **The wiki's first ingested control-barrier-function source**, which partially closes a gap named a year ago by [awesome-physical-ai](sources/awesome-physical-ai-github.md): *"the wiki's safety folder is alignment/mech-interp-oriented; **robot control safety** is a real gap."*
- **Mechanism**: safe set = zero-superlevel set of `h(z)`; enforce `ḣ ≥ −α(h)` as one linear inequality in the QP and the set becomes **forward-invariant** — a claim about all future states, where the deployed envelope's hard inequalities only make a claim about *this tick*. Manipulator constraints are mostly **relative degree 2** under torque control, so they need High-Order CBFs; the paper tabulates RD per constraint type and control mode.
- **The contribution beyond "CBFs in an OSC" is the objective, and it is the part worth stealing.** The standard filter minimizes `‖u − u_nom‖²` over the **raw control input**. The authors argue that is the wrong quantity whenever there is a task hierarchy, and name three failure modes: joint-space metric for an operational-space task; operational-space metric that ignores the null-space joint task; and a **torque** metric when the task is defined over **position**. **Task consistency** = *"the filter should minimally modify an output that reflects the task and hierarchy definition"* — so the objective is deviation in operational-space and null-space joint **accelerations**. Generalizes past robotics: every minimally-invasive filter has to answer *"minimal in what?"*, and the actuator command is rarely the right answer. Filed in the glossary.
- **The numbers are the reason it's deployable, not a demo.** 7-DoF Franka Panda on a **laptop-class i7 NUC**, in **Python**: 168 concurrent CBF constraints at **2.94 kHz** (torque, mean) / 2.25 kHz (5th pct); single-constraint cases at 6–10 kHz; **>400 constraints at 1 kHz**, **>1000 above 100 Hz** for velocity control. Implementation is **CBFpy** — JAX autodiff for the barrier functions (Lie derivatives as JVPs), JIT to XLA, primal-dual interior-point QP also in JAX.
- **Consequence for the [control-rate ladder](syntheses/platforms/control-rate-ladder.md), which now carries three OSCBF rows**: the *safety* layer has three orders of magnitude more headroom than the policy it guards. The constraint layer is not what limits this stack.
- **The dynamics point is the sharpest empirical result** (§V-D). Prior CBF-for-manipulator work assumes a low-level controller can always realize a commanded safe velocity — *"we emphasize that this is **not** the case for dynamic motions, with torque limits."* On a fast trajectory into the unsafe set, **both** velocity- and torque-CBF robots stay safe, but the velocity one visibly **loses tracking** because its safe command is instantaneously infeasible. Safety survives; performance does not. Only the full second-order formulation keeps both.
- **It adds a constraint class the deployed envelope doesn't have**: **singularity avoidance**, as `h = μ(q) − ε` on Yoshikawa's manipulability index, demonstrated at `μ = 1e−2` on hardware. Not collision safety — exactly the failure where a diff-IK QP's joint velocities blow up.
- **Hardware**: 1 kHz real-time torque interface, four teleoperated experiments, approaches the safety boundary within **6 mm**, and *"effectively zero mismatch between the hardware data and simulation, save for a miniscule amount of latency (50 ms) from the teleoperation interface."* Tuning claim worth noting in a literature where tuning is the standing complaint: `α = α₂ = 10`, `W_j = W_o = I` for **every** experiment.
- **Recorded the limits at the weight the authors state them.** (1) With many CBFs plus input constraints the QP goes **infeasible**, and the relaxed version *"enforces (but does not **guarantee**) safety in most cases"* — so the proof weakens precisely in the many-constraint regime the headline numbers occupy. (2) Input constraints in the QP *"do not guarantee forward invariance"* unless designed into the CBF. (3) **No learned policy is ever run** — all experiments are teleop or scripted. (4) **Obstacles are given, not perceived.**
- **The finding that spans both pages**: the two halves of this problem have never been put together. **Stanford has the guarantee and no policy; MIT/TRI has the policy and no guarantee** — same robot (Franka Panda), same rate (~1 kHz), same architectural slot. OSCBF's own future work is the missing experiment: *"deploying the learned policies with OSCBF maintaining whole-body safety."* Filed as a backlog item, together with the observation that **what a safety filter costs a policy in task success is unmeasured anywhere in this corpus** — and per the [success-rate audit](syntheses/platforms/vla-success-rate-audit.md), measuring it honestly needs ~1,000 rollouts.
- **And the gap neither side closes is the same one**: keep-out boxes and sphere decompositions are authored offline on both coasts. **Real-time perception → constraint geometry** is what separates either from safety in an unstructured home.
- [Marco Pavone](entities/marco-pavone.md) earns a page at three ingested sources, and they form a line rather than a coincidence: **make learned components admissible to classical control machinery** — either by giving a learned dynamics model the structure a controller synthesis needs ([SD-LQR](sources/learning-control-oriented-dynamical-structure.md), ICML 2023) or by wrapping a learned policy in a filter with an invariance proof (this).
- Also updated: [operational space control](concepts/robotics/operational-space-control.md) (new CBF section + a deployed-vs-OSCBF comparison table), [robot safety standards](concepts/robotics/robot-safety-standards.md) (the first artifact here a physical-safety conformity argument could rest on — and why it isn't one yet), control-rate ladder, glossary (**CBF**, **task consistency**), index, backlog.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | PACS — what a safety filter actually costs a learned policy
- Created [PACS paper](sources/pacs-paper.md) (Römer, Balletshofer, Thumm, [Pavone](entities/marco-pavone.md), Schoellig & Althoff; TUM MIRMI + Stanford; arXiv 2511.06385, **ICRA 2026**; PDF into `raw/`) and the hub concept [safety filters for learned policies](concepts/robotics/safety-filters.md).
- **This closes an open question filed hours earlier, and corrects the claim that came with it.** The [operational space control](concepts/robotics/operational-space-control.md) page said the cost of a safety filter to a policy's task success was *"unmeasured anywhere in this corpus."* True of the corpus, wrong as an impression of the field — it was measured, in ICRA 2026, by a group including the senior author of the [OSCBF](sources/oscbf-paper.md) paper ingested the same afternoon.
- **The finding reorders the axis I had been using.** I filed OSCBF as an upgrade along *guarantee strength* — hand-written constraints → forward invariance. PACS shows that axis does not predict task success. **Path-consistent vs path-deviating does.** On robomimic with a moving obstacle: a **CBF filter scores 0.04** average success (zero on two of three tasks); **PACS scores 0.72** against **0.70** for the identical pipeline with the failsafe switched off. Same robot, same constraints, same class of proof.
- **The mechanism is distribution shift, not control.** A reactive filter enforces safety by moving the robot *off its intended path* — and a behavior-cloned policy that lands off the demonstration manifold is **out of distribution and cannot recover**. The paper shows it rather than asserting it: end-effector traces plotted over the training distribution, with the CBF pushing into unvisited regions and the policy failing there. **Every classical safety filter is designed against a dynamics criterion; none is designed against "will the policy still recognize this state."** Those come apart precisely when the filtered object is learned.
- **PACS's actual construction**: integrate the **action chunk** into waypoints → solve a time-optimal, jerk-limited **intended trajectory** through them (Ruckig) → each safety tick, verify [intended ∥ failsafe-stop] by **set-based reachability** (SaRA) → execute the intended motion, or fall back to the last verified failsafe. Safe by induction. Enabling contribution is the chunk handling: prior path-consistent filters take a single goal with zero terminal velocity, so on a chunked policy they stop at every waypoint or skip waypoints — **worth +28% task success to fix**.
- **The subtlest design point, and the one I'd have missed**: observations are RGB + joint angles, **no velocity**, deliberately — *so that slowing the robot down is not itself an out-of-distribution state*. The safety mechanism imposes a constraint on **how the policy is trained**. "Bolt a filter on afterwards" is not quite available.
- **Constraints come from ISO/TS 15066, with numbers** — the wiki's first ingested case of a learned policy held to a machinery-safety standard's actual thresholds. **SSM** (no contact; complete stop if a collision is reachable) for the coexistence task; **PFL** for collaboration, at **0.014 J** hand/constrained, **0.265 J** hand/unconstrained, and **0.001 J** for a fork near a **head/eye**. Sourced from surrogate-injury literature, not tuned. Filed on [robot safety standards](concepts/robotics/robot-safety-standards.md) and [assistive robotics](concepts/robotics/assistive-robotics.md).
- **The hardware table is the paper.** Franka FR3, three HRI tasks (sorting beside a person, handover from a moving hand, **feeding**), 30 rollouts each, diffusion policy **and [SmolVLA](entities/smolvla.md)**: unsafeguarded policies average **0.79 task success, 0.00 safe success**, violating constraints in **56% of all timesteps** and in *every* rollout. With PACS: **0.80 / 0.80 / zero**. Task success and safety are close to independent, which is the argument for an external mechanism in one table.
- **`Safe success` now sits in [robot policy evaluation](concepts/robotics/robot-policy-evaluation.md)**, with the implication stated plainly: **every success rate in this wiki was collected without any safety constraint being checked.** Where a human is in the workspace, task success alone is not a deployment-relevant number.
- **It is also cheaper and faster than what it replaces**: 0.20 ms per safety step vs 0.64 ms for the CBF baseline; and time-parameterizing the chunk under real kinematic limits cut task duration **25.2 s → 21.7 s (−14%)** with +13% end-effector speed at identical success. A safety layer that makes the robot faster is not the usual shape.
- **Recorded the caveats at their real weight**: **n = 30** per hardware cell (0.77-vs-0.80 is noise; 0.00-vs-0.80 safe success is not), the quantitative **feeding runs use a printed face with a cut-out mouth**, object poses are **measured, not perceived** (bounded-error state estimate assumed — the same standing gap as OSCBF and the TRI envelope), and **static clutter is future work** — the mirror image of OSCBF, which handles hundreds of static pairs and never faces distribution shift. **Nothing covers both hazard classes.**
- **Not a dispute between camps.** PACS's CBF baseline is Singletary et al. 2022, and the paper cites OSCBF in the same related-work list — with **Pavone on both**. The two "consistency" ideas are complementary and operate at different scopes: **task consistency** (don't inject excess null-space motion *at an instant*) and **path consistency** (don't leave the demonstration manifold *over a trajectory*). A task-consistent CBF is still path-deviating.
- New hub page [safety filters for learned policies](concepts/robotics/safety-filters.md) carries the three-way comparison, the literature's own taxonomy (optimization-based vs reachability-based; post-hoc vs injected into the denoising process), and the three things none of the three do: **perception, semantic safety, certification**.
- Backlog gained the uningested **runtime-monitoring cluster** PACS cites — Agia/Sinha/Pavone/Bohg (CoRL 2025), **Xu et al. (RSS 2025, TRI: detecting failures without failure data)**, Römer et al. (NeurIPS 2025) — three 2025 papers on knowing when a generative policy is going wrong, adjacent to this wiki's evaluation thread and absent from it.
- Also updated: [operational space control](concepts/robotics/operational-space-control.md) (the reordering, as a warning callout), [Marco Pavone](entities/marco-pavone.md) (4 sources; a line correcting itself), [SmolVLA](entities/smolvla.md), control-rate ladder, glossary (**safety filter, reachability analysis, SSM, PFL**), index.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | Sentinel + FAIL-Detect — the detection half, and the two papers disagree
- Created [Sentinel](sources/sentinel-paper.md) (Agia, Sinha, Yang, Cao, Antonova, [Pavone](entities/marco-pavone.md), Bohg; Stanford, CoRL 2024), [FAIL-Detect](sources/fail-detect-paper.md) (Xu et al., **[TRI](entities/tri.md)**, RSS 2025), and the concept page [runtime failure detection](concepts/robotics/runtime-failure-detection.md). Both PDFs into `raw/`.
- **This is the third distinct mechanism a deployed policy needs, and the wiki now has all three separated cleanly**: a **safety filter** asks *will this action hurt someone*; a **runtime monitor** asks *is this rollout going to succeed*; **offline evaluation** asks *does this policy work at all*. The filter cannot see a policy confidently doing the wrong thing; the monitor cannot stop the arm swinging through the table. **Nothing in this corpus runs more than one of them at once.**
- **Both train on successes only, and the reason is the strongest single figure in either paper**: [FAIL-Detect](sources/fail-detect-paper.md) shows **six qualitatively different failures from one policy on one pick-and-place task** — slipped early, slipped late, tilted three ways, never picked up. Any detector needing labelled failures is fighting a combinatorial problem.
- **The finding that generalizes furthest: state atypicality is not policy failure.** Embedding-similarity OOD detectors (CLIP, ResNet, the policy's own encoder) score **TNR = 0.00** on Sentinel's OOD tests — they flag *every* unfamiliar rollout, including the ones where the policy generalizes and succeeds. Filed on [robot policy evaluation](concepts/robotics/robot-policy-evaluation.md): any evaluation treating "OOD" as a proxy for "will fail" inherits that error, and generalization is the thing a generalist policy was deployed for.
- **STAC's mechanism is the one to remember, and it falls out of action chunking.** A chunked policy queried at `t` and again at `t+k` produces **two distributions over the same future timesteps**, from different observations. Their statistical distance (MMD/KL) is a direct measure of the policy changing its mind — no extra model, no failure data, and well-posed under multimodality where comparing two *sampled* trajectories is meaningless. Cumulate, threshold by conformal quantile, done.
- **Sentinel's organizing idea is a taxonomy with opposite time requirements**: **erratic** failures (jitter, conflicting modes) must be caught fast → STAC, cheap; **task-progression** failures (stalling, drifting, confidently wrong) *cannot* be caught fast → a **VLM doing chain-of-thought video QA** at ~14 s, which is fine because they are not urgent. Defining one category as the complement of the other makes the combination a logical OR: **+18% over either alone**, 93% TPR on progression failures for +7% FPR.
- **A VLM given a single image is useless as a monitor (TNR 0.00); the same model over video reaches 0.91.** It also needs elapsed time and the horizon to judge whether the current *rate* finishes in time. Worth carrying into any "just ask a VLM" design.
