- **`logpZO` is FAIL-Detect's trick and it generalizes past robotics**: fit a normalizing flow to successful-rollout observations, then instead of scoring likelihood in observation space (needs the divergence integrated along the ODE — badly behaved in high dimensions), run the **forward** ODE and check whether the encoding looks like the Gaussian noise it should be. The score is **`‖Z‖²`**.
- **Recorded as a contradiction, because it is one.** Sentinel calls STAC's cost *"negligible."* FAIL-Detect measures **1.45 s/timestep vs 0.033–0.04 s for `logpZO` — 36–44×** — reports STAC's detection time as *"consistently exceed[ing] practical limits, surpassing the average success trajectory time,"* and **omits STAC from its hardware experiments entirely** as too slow for real time (it also drops Sentinel's VLM half for the same reason). Both can be literally true; the **operational verdicts differ, and it is the industrial lab saying no**. Caveat noted on the other side: FAIL-Detect reproduces STAC with **PushT hyperparameters** on other tasks and without its VLM.
- **The honest accuracy numbers are modest and worth keeping visible**: FAIL-Detect averages **~78% balanced accuracy in simulation, ~72% on hardware**. This is a viable formulation, not a solved problem — and its authors concede the learned scores often read **robot state rather than pixels** (gripper open/closed), which is a weaker claim than "we detect OOD conditions."
- **Both guarantees run the same direction, and it is the convenient one.** Conformal calibration bounds the **false-alarm rate**; bounding *misses* would need failure data, which is the assumption both methods exist to avoid. **The statistical guarantee available at runtime protects throughput, not people.** Filed on the concept page and on policy evaluation.
- **Neither predicts** — Sentinel says so outright: *"not targeted at predicting failures before they occur."* The one paper in the cluster that does (Römer et al., NeurIPS 2025) stays on the backlog.
- **And neither recovers.** Both raise a flag and stop. What happens next — handoff, retry, replan, safe park — is unaddressed, which is the same [empty execution rail](syntheses/agents/guardrails-for-robot-agents.md) this wiki keeps finding from the agent side. That gap is now named on three pages and is the cheapest concrete thing left in this whole thread.
- **Neither runs on a generalist policy.** All experiments are single-task diffusion/flow policies; [LBMs](concepts/learning/large-behavior-models.md) and VLAs are untested, and STAC needs cheap batch sampling a large autoregressive VLA cannot give. TRI funds Sentinel *and* wrote FAIL-Detect *and* ships the LBM program — the pieces are in one building.
- [Marco Pavone](entities/marco-pavone.md) reaches **five ingested sources** and three distinct attacks on one problem: give a learned model the structure a controller needs (SD-LQR), bound it with a filter (OSCBF), measure what the filter costs and fix it (PACS), and detect when it fails anyway (Sentinel).
- Glossary: **conformal prediction**, **STAC**. Also updated [safety filters](concepts/robotics/safety-filters.md) (prevention vs detection), index, backlog.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | FOREWARN + FIPER, and the synthesis that closes the runtime thread
- Created [FOREWARN](sources/forewarn-paper.md) (Wu, Tian, Swamy & Bajcsy; CMU), [FIPER](sources/fiper-paper.md) (Römer, Kobras, Worbis & Schoellig; TUM, **NeurIPS 2025**), and the synthesis [Prevention, detection, intervention](syntheses/platforms/prevention-detection-intervention.md). Both PDFs into `raw/`.

### FOREWARN — the intervention layer
- **Reframes the problem**: most runtime failures are **mode-selection failures, not capability failures** — *"the base policy may already contain the 'right' behavior mode within its distribution… but due to putting too much probability mass on an undesired mode, the robot does not reliably choose the correct action plan."* So don't retrain, **re-pick**.
- Sample K plans → predict each outcome with a **frozen [DreamerV3](entities/dreamer.md)** latent world model → **LoRA-tuned Llama-3.2-11B narrates the predicted latents** through a linear adapter into token space → the same VLM picks the best against the task description. Base success **0.30/0.20/0.10 → 0.80/0.70/0.70** on three real Franka tasks.
- **Predicted latents beat ground-truth images as VLM input: 0.82 vs 0.52** for GPT-4o shown the *actual* future frames. A frontier VLM does not reliably read fine-grained contact detail (handle vs rim vs interior) off video; a small fine-tuned model reading a world model's imagination does.
- **The generalization result is the one that matters, and it is about representation.** A transformer classifier over the *same* predicted latents ties FOREWARN on trained task descriptions (0.80/0.70/0.70) and **collapses to 0.00/0.10/0.20** on novel ones ("the handle is covered with oil"). Routing the decision through **natural-language narration** rather than asking the VLM for a category is what preserves open-world reasoning — *"essentially degrades to a traditional end-to-end model"* otherwise. **Text is not the interface here; it is the representation in which generalization survives.**
- **The structural convergence worth carrying, and neither paper states it in general form**: [PACS](sources/pacs-paper.md) brakes along the intended path, FOREWARN selects among the policy's own samples — both **intervene by choosing, not by correcting**, and both are in-distribution by construction. One came from safety, one from intent-alignment. Filed as the rule that spans the synthesis.
- Added a **new function** to the [world-model functional taxonomy](concepts/world-models/world-model-functional-taxonomy.md): world model as **runtime verifier** — not renderer, simulator or planner. Note the different data requirement: it is deliberately trained on the base policy's **failures as well as successes**, because a verifier must predict bad outcomes too.

### FIPER — prediction, and a metric worth keeping
- **Two signals, conjoined**: consecutive **OOD observations** via random network distillation run **inside the policy's own embedding space** (RND-OE) **AND** prolonged **action-chunk entropy** (ACE). The **AND is the structural inverse of [Sentinel](sources/sentinel-paper.md)'s OR** — union to raise recall vs conjunction to suppress alarms on situations the policy can handle.
- **Entropy, not variance, and the argument is clean**: IL multimodality is **discrete** (pick A, B or C; grasp from the side or above), so a healthy policy legitimately emits very different actions for one observation and variance says nothing. What signals trouble is whether the modes are **sharp**.
- **Second technical critique of STAC in two papers.** FIPER: STAC *"typically associates timesteps at which the policy **decides on a behavior mode** with high uncertainty"* — it fires when a multimodal policy commits, which is normal. [FAIL-Detect](sources/fail-detect-paper.md) went after its speed; this goes after its semantics.
- **TWA — a metric contribution that outlives the method.** Standard metrics do not reward prediction: waiting until the last timestep scores high accuracy, flagging everything at t=0 scores a perfect detection time. **Timestep-wise accuracy** credits a true positive with `1 − DT`. Filed on [robot policy evaluation](concepts/robotics/robot-policy-evaluation.md) alongside the **Success-ID / Success-OOD / Fail-ID / Fail-OOD** protocol, which turns "OOD ≠ failure" from an observation into a measurement — *the gap between Success-OOD and Fail-ID is the thing to measure*.
- Results: average **accuracy 0.78 / TWA 0.65** vs 0.69 (`logpZO`) and 0.68 (STAC). Real tasks show the biggest margin (PushChair 0.96). **First head-to-head in this cluster** — it reimplements both prior methods as baselines.

### The synthesis
- [Prevention, detection, intervention](syntheses/platforms/prevention-detection-intervention.md) pulls six sources into one argument: three layers answering three different questions, and **nothing in this corpus runs more than one of them at once**.
- **The rule**: *an intervention that stays inside the policy's own output distribution costs almost nothing; one that leaves it costs almost everything* (0.04 vs 0.72). With the three scopes of "minimal in what?" — **task consistency** (OSCBF), **path consistency** (PACS), **selection not correction** (FOREWARN).
- **Four things wrong with every layer at once**: they all consume hazard geometry they don't produce (**perception is the shared blocker**); every guarantee is conformal and bounds **false alarms rather than misses**, because that is all you can get from successful data — *the guarantees protect throughput, not people*; detection plateaus at **~0.78** across three groups while prevention is near-solved, because **physics is enumerable and task success is not**; and at n = 20–50 per cell, only order-of-magnitude effects survive.
- **The original finding — a hazard nobody has hit because nobody has run two layers together.** A path-consistent safety filter **braking to a stop for a human** is *the same observable behavior* as the stalling failure a task-progression monitor is built to catch. PACS's correct action is Sentinel's positive class. Generalized: **a monitor watching a filtered policy is not watching the policy** — it sees the composition, whose healthy behavior includes states that look pathological in isolation. The filter has to *tell* the monitor it is intervening, and neither literature has that interface. Same collision for steering: FOREWARN forces a mode commitment every cycle, which is exactly what STAC flags.
- Practical upshots recorded: pick interventions that never leave the output distribution; budget perception separately; instrument the interfaces before you need them; report **safe success**; and don't read a conformal guarantee as a safety guarantee.

## [2026-08-16] correction | Targeted literature search falsified three "nobody has done it" claims
- **Prompted by a direct challenge.** After the FOREWARN/FIPER ingest I asserted that the runtime thread's remaining gaps were *"no longer readable — buildable"*: no monitor on a generalist policy, no perception-driven constraint geometry, no crossing of the monitor designs, no detection→recovery loop. I flagged the caveat that this was a claim about my search coverage rather than about the field. **The challenge was to actually check. Three of the four were wrong.**
- **Wrong #1 — monitors on generalist policies.** [SAFE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09937) (Gu, Ju, Sun, Gilitschenski, **Nishimura**, **Itkina**, Shkurti; **NeurIPS 2025**) does multitask failure detection for **OpenVLA, π₀ and π₀-FAST** from the VLA's **internal features**, conformally calibrated, **generalizing to unseen tasks** — motivated by exactly the gap the wiki named (*"existing failure detectors are trained and tested only on one or a few specific tasks"*). Nishimura and Itkina co-wrote [FAIL-Detect](sources/fail-detect-paper.md), so the group I described as monitoring single-task policies had already published the generalist version. Also found: **VLA-FAIL**, **Hide-and-Seek in Trajectories** (which critiques SAFE), **VLAConf**, **ActProbe**.
- **Wrong #2 — perception as "the single shared blocker."** [Latent Safety Filters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.00935) (Nakamura, **Bajcsy** et al.) run **Hamilton–Jacobi reachability inside a generative world model's latent space**, so constraint specification becomes *classification in latent space* over raw RGB, targeting hazards *"hard — if not impossible — to write down by hand"*: preventing a Franka from **spilling a bag** or **toppling clutter**. Two follow-ups exist (uncertainty-aware; partial observability). Same lab as [FOREWARN](sources/forewarn-paper.md) — a connection the wiki missed while ingesting FOREWARN itself.
- **Wrong #3 — "detection is not recovery."** [Rewind-IL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16683) (Zheng, Seenivasan, Johnson-Roberson & Zhi, 2026-04) detects with **TIDE** (chunk-overlap consistency, i.e. STAC's signal) under **split conformal prediction**, then **rewinds to the latest VLM-verified safe checkpoint** and restarts inference. Real hardware, long-horizon tasks. It simultaneously answers the *"nobody has crossed the two monitor designs"* item.
- **Survived, with low confidence** — the claim that a safety filter braking for a human is indistinguishable, to a progress monitor, from a stalled policy. No source found names that composition. But the ingredients are documented separately (over-conservative filters under partial observability, 2510.06492 / 2606.02562; monitors firing on benign situations), and three neighbouring claims fell on first contact, so this one is held loosely.
- **Two adjacent works the synthesis should not have implied did not exist**: **Christopher Agia's Stanford dissertation** *Deployment-Time Reliability of Learned Robot Policies* (2026-03, 182 pp) — [Sentinel](sources/sentinel-paper.md)'s first author, cutting the problem as monitoring / **policy interpretability via influence functions** / long-horizon coordination, and **the wiki has no coverage at all of tracing a runtime failure back to its training data**; and the **cross-layer survey** 2606.05660, which organizes safety by planning-time / policy-time / execution-time and argues risks *accumulate* in one closed loop. Same instinct as [the synthesis](syntheses/platforms/prevention-detection-intervention.md), different cut — **the synthesis is not the first to argue the layers interact**, and now says so.
- **Corrections applied in place** rather than by deletion: warning callouts on [runtime failure detection](concepts/robotics/runtime-failure-detection.md) (two), [safety filters](concepts/robotics/safety-filters.md) (one), and [the synthesis](syntheses/platforms/prevention-detection-intervention.md) (three, plus an "adjacent work" section); falsified backlog items struck with the reason; a new ingest queue filed.
- **The process lesson, recorded because it will recur**: *"no ingested source does X"* and *"nobody does X"* are different claims, and the wiki must write the first. Every gap statement produced from a corpus of ~950 pages is a statement about the corpus. In an area publishing this fast, a gap claim should carry the search that backs it or an explicit scope marker — and I should run the search before writing the claim, not after being asked.

## [2026-08-16] correction | JetPack 7.2 — the Orin tiers were still filed as JetPack 6
- **Question asked of the [XLeRobot onboard-compute page](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md): have these Jetson boards moved to JetPack 7?** They have, and the wiki had not.
- **JetPack 7.2 (Jetson Linux r39.2), released 2026-06-01, brings JetPack 7 to the whole Orin family** — AGX Orin, Orin NX, Orin Nano — on **Ubuntu 24.04 / kernel 6.8 / CUDA 13.0**, unifying the toolchain with Thor. **JetPack 7.2.1** followed **2026-08-12**. Live-web facts verified 2026-08-16 (NVIDIA JetPack page, Connect Tech, JetsonHacks, NVIDIA/Seeed forums); **no JetPack 7.2 release note is ingested**, which is now the open item.
- **The root of the staleness was [the JetPack entity page](entities/jetpack.md)**, which stated *"Orin-class modules remain on the JetPack 6.x track… JetPack 6 and JetPack 7 are **parallel, not sequential**."* True when written (2026-05-17), false since June 1. Four maintained pages inherited it: the [onboard-compute synthesis](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md) (SDK row), the [module ladder](syntheses/platforms/jetson-module-ladder-power-performance.md) (SDK column, all Orin rows), the [Orin Nano entity](entities/jetson-orin-nano.md), and the [flash howto](syntheses/projects/jetson-orin-nano-flash-howto.md).
- **Two consequences that bite a build rather than a spec sheet**, and they are why this is worth more than a version bump:
  - **The Orin Nano dev kit no longer has an SD-card image.** From 7.2 it is a **unified ISO written to a USB stick**, which then installs to microSD or NVMe — *"do not flash the Jetson ISO to a microSD card"* — defaulting to **Super Mode** flashing config. **The [flash howto](syntheses/projects/jetson-orin-nano-flash-howto.md) documents the JetPack 6 flow**, including a microSD path that no longer exists, and now carries a warning to that effect. JetPack 6 → 7 is a **full reflash**, not an apt upgrade.
  - **Third-party carriers lag NVIDIA by weeks, and one change is a silent camera trap.** Seeed shipped **JetPack 7.2.0 / R39.2.0 for the reComputer J401 and J501 on 2026-06-30** — the carriers this wiki recommends for the Orin NX tier — and **R39.2.0 moves to a 22-pin Jetson CSI connector spec where R36.4.3 used 24-pin, so camera device trees need updating.** AGX Orin 32 GB carrier support was still an open question in that thread.
- **No recommendation changed.** The tiering, power budget, and model-rate arguments on the compute pages are unaffected; what changed is the software baseline, and the fact that **the Orin/Thor split is now a hardware-generation split rather than a toolchain split** — which quietly *strengthens* the "Orin NX is the sweet spot" case, since it no longer means living on an older SDK line.
- **Source pages left alone deliberately** ([JetPack 6.2.2 release](sources/nvidia-jetpack-6-2-2-release.md), [R36.5 notes](sources/nvidia-jetson-linux-r36-5-release-notes.md), the Seeed guides): a source page records what a source said at its date. Only *maintained* pages — entities, syntheses — were corrected, each with a dated callout rather than a silent edit.
- **Second staleness lesson in one day**, and the same shape as this morning's literature correction: **the wiki's facts about a fast-moving vendor decay on a ~2-month timescale, and nothing in the workflow re-checks them.** A version-bump sweep over the Jetson/JetPack cluster is now a standing backlog item.

## [2026-08-16] ingest | JetPack 7.2 primary source — and it corrected my correction
- Created [NVIDIA JetPack 7.2 with Jetson Linux 39.2](sources/nvidia-jetpack-7-2-release.md), the primary source behind this morning's version correction. Extracted by **curl + tag stripping** — WebFetch returns a summary of this page, not the component table ([docs-site gotcha](sources/nvidia-jetpack-docs-index.md) again).
- **It confirms the correction and fixes a number in it.** I had recorded **CUDA 13.0** from NVIDIA's landing page and two secondary reports that agreed. The release page shows **CUDA 13.2.1**; the 13.0 figure belongs to a different sentence — *"Jetson Thor now supports a unified CUDA 13.0 installation across all Arm targets"*, a statement about **SBSA/Arm-target unification**, not this bundle's version. Three secondary sources agreeing did not make it right.
- **Three facts secondary reporting missed entirely, all of which matter more than the version number:**
  - **Isaac ROS is listed "Coming soon"** on 7.2, and **Jetson Platform Services N/A**. For a ROS robot that is the decisive line in the release: **moving an Orin to 7.2 today means giving up Isaac ROS.** The [onboard-compute page](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md) has described the Thor tier as "JetPack 7 / Isaac ROS 4" since June — that is a **roadmap pairing, not a shipping one**, and it now says so.
  - **`overlay_pcie.tbz2`** — *"fixes an intermittent boot issue caused by initialization failures on some Jetson Orin Nano and Orin NX modules during power cycles or reboots."* An intermittent boot failure **on power cycle** is close to the worst failure mode an untethered robot can have, and the fix is a file you have to know to apply.
  - **AGX Orin 32 GB Super Mode (MAXN_SUPER): 200 → 241 TOPS.**
- **That last one inverts a finding on the [module ladder](syntheses/platforms/jetson-module-ladder-power-performance.md).** The page's efficiency table had AGX Orin 32 GB as the one dip in the Orin ladder — *less* efficient than Orin NX 16 GB, 3.3 vs 3.9 TOPS/W. At 241 TOPS in the same 60 W envelope it is **~4.0**, edging past the Orin NX and removing the dip. **Recorded with the hedge it deserves**: 4.0 vs 3.9 is inside the noise of a metric built from headline TOPS over max-mode wattage, and the Orin NX recommendation never rested on efficiency — it rests on the **10–40 W envelope, drop-in carrier compatibility, and price**, none of which moved. What the page can no longer claim is that the 32 GB AGX buys memory and IO *at an efficiency penalty*.
- Other 7.2 contents now recorded: **MIG on Thor T5000** (tech preview), **SIPL API v2.0.0** unifying GMSL and CoE camera paths, official **Yocto** recipes via OE4T (images for AGX Thor, AGX Orin, Orin Nano — **not** Orin NX), DeepStream 9.1, Holoscan 3.9.0, Vulkan 1.4, **OpenWF Display: not supported**, and native single-command **[NemoClaw](entities/nemoclaw.md)** install — a notable placement for NemoClaw, shipping as a headline feature of the BSP release.
- Propagated to [JetPack](entities/jetpack.md) (a full 7.2 section with a 6.2.2-vs-7.2 component table), [Orin Nano](entities/jetson-orin-nano.md), the [module ladder](syntheses/platforms/jetson-module-ladder-power-performance.md), the [onboard-compute synthesis](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md), and the [flash howto](syntheses/projects/jetson-orin-nano-flash-howto.md).
- **The methodological point, since this is the second correction-of-a-correction today**: the earlier fix was right in substance and wrong in a detail *because it rested on three secondary sources that agreed with each other*. Agreement among secondaries is not corroboration when they are all paraphrasing the same page. **Ingest the primary when the claim is going to be quoted in a buying or flashing decision.**

## [2026-08-17] schema | Ingest workflow — primary sources for decision-grade claims
- Added a **"Primary sources for decision-grade claims"** subsection to the Ingest workflow in `CLAUDE.md`, promoting yesterday's methodological finding from a one-off log note to a standing convention.
- **The rule:** when a vendor or spec claim will be quoted in a buying, flashing, or build decision, ingest the primary as its own source page and don't cite secondary coverage for it. **Count distinct origins, not distinct URLs** — three pages paraphrasing one release note are one source.
- Records the two failure modes from the [JetPack 7.2 correction](#2026-08-16-ingest--jetpack-72-primary-source--and-it-corrected-my-correction): **scope loss** (paraphrase keeps the noun phrase, drops what it was bound to — "CUDA 13.0" was a statement about Arm/SBSA target unification, not the bundle version) and **omission**, which cost more (Isaac ROS "Coming soon", the `overlay_pcie.tbz2` boot fix, and the AGX TOPS revision appeared in no secondary reporting).
- **The trigger is document type, not doubt.** I had no reason to doubt the CUDA figure. The signal is whether you're summarizing a document's *contents* versus quoting one fact from it — release notes, spec tables, and datasheets are always the former.
- Cross-referenced the docs-site extraction gotcha ([NVIDIA JetPack docs index](sources/nvidia-jetpack-docs-index.md)), since the primary is usually the page WebFetch handles worst.

## [2026-08-17] sweep | Jetson/JetPack cluster version sweep — three primaries, three corrections
- **Cleared the standing backlog item** "sweep the Jetson/JetPack cluster for other version drift." Method was the convention committed this morning: for every claim that feeds a buying or flashing decision, go to the vendor primary. Three new source pages, seven maintained pages corrected. **All three corrections were of claims that came from secondary reporting, including two of my own from yesterday.**
- **New primaries:** [Jetson Linux 39.2.0 GA release notes](sources/nvidia-jetson-linux-r39-2-release-notes.md) (PDF, in `raw/`), [Isaac ROS release notes + supported platforms](sources/isaac-ros-release-notes-and-platforms.md), [Seeed J401 flash guide](sources/seeed-j401-flash-jetpack.md).

### 1. Isaac ROS 4.x dropped Jetson Orin entirely — and Thor's Isaac ROS 4 is *shipping*, not roadmap
- Yesterday's ingest read "Isaac ROS: Coming soon" on the [JetPack 7.2 page](sources/nvidia-jetpack-7-2-release.md) and concluded the Thor column's "JetPack 7 / Isaac ROS 4" was **"a roadmap pairing, not a shipping one."** That was wrong. **Isaac ROS 4.0 shipped 2025-10-24** with Thor + JetPack 7.0 support; **4.2** (2026-02-19) added JetPack 7.1 and the T4000 SKU; **4.5.0** (2026-07-06) is current.
- The real finding is worse and points the other way. The [supported-platform table](sources/isaac-ros-release-notes-and-platforms.md) — "the **only** hardware and software combinations that Isaac ROS tests and officially supports" — lists **Thor T5000/T4000 on JetPack 7.1**, **x86_64 / Ubuntu 24.04 / CUDA 13**, and **DGX Spark**. **No Orin appears, on any JetPack.** The last Orin-supporting release is **3.2 Update 1 (2025-01-16)** on JetPack 6.1/6.2, Ubuntu 22.04, CUDA 12.6, **ROS 2 Humble**. 4.x is a **Jazzy** line, so it is also a distro migration.
- **Consequence for this wiki's buying advice:** the Orin tiers face a closed door either way — stay on JetPack 6.2 and hold a frozen Isaac ROS 3.2, or move to 7.2 and have none. "Coming soon" was a schedule note; this is a generational break. Filed on [Isaac ROS](entities/isaac-ros.md), [JetPack](entities/jetpack.md), [nvblox](entities/nvblox.md), [Jetson Thor](entities/jetson-thor.md), and the [onboard-compute synthesis](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md).

### 2. The unified ISO does *not* default to Super Mode — it preserves the profile already set
- Recorded yesterday from secondary coverage: "the ISO defaults to Super Mode flashing config." The [r39.2 release notes](sources/nvidia-jetson-linux-r39-2-release-notes.md), issue **6279443**, say the opposite: Orin Nano units updated by ISO *"continue to use the same profile that was set before update. Units will not default to 'Super' mode… To use 'Super' mode, you must flash the target using a Linux host or SDKM."*
- Two more flashing facts from the same primary: **issue 6266271** — the QSPI **capsule-update prompt must be answered `y`**, or the ISO install fails on incompatibility with the older QSPI (a matching *"Capsule staged 5 times but version not bumped"* failure is reported on Seeed J4012/J401); and the host-OS matrix, where Seeed is **stricter than NVIDIA** (Ubuntu 24.04 flash-only, use 20.04/22.04 for host dev components). Corrected on the [flash howto](syntheses/projects/jetson-orin-nano-flash-howto.md), [JetPack](entities/jetpack.md), and the [onboard-compute synthesis](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md).

### 3. Two new constraints under the Orin NX recommendation — one thermal, one a crash bug
- **Carrier thermals cap Super Mode.** Seeed's own flash guide for the **J401** — the carrier under the reComputer J4012 this wiki recommends — says *"if you are using an Orin NX 16GB/8GB module, **do not enable MAXN SUPER mode**. The cooling capacity of the reComputer J401 carrier board is insufficient to support it."* Seeed separately markets the **Super J4012 at 157 TOPS in Super MAXN**. Same silicon, two sanctioned ceilings; the variable is the carrier. **So the 157 TOPS figure on the [module ladder](syntheses/platforms/jetson-module-ladder-power-performance.md) and the [onboard-compute page](syntheses/platforms/jetson-onboard-compute-xlerobot.md) is contingent on which Seeed box was bought** — and the Robotics J30/40 (the battery-robot pick, quoted at 157 TOPS / 40 W) has no thermal primary in the wiki at all.
- **The low end of the power envelope has an open crash bug.** Issue **6236259**: dropping EMC below Fmax via `nvpmodel.service` during systemd init "can cause system crashes upon reboot," affecting **Orin NX 16/8 GB @ 10 W**, **Orin Nano 8 GB @ 7 W**, AGX Orin @ 15 W — "especially noticeable when a display is connected." The Orin NX is recommended here *for* its 10–40 W envelope, and **10 W is precisely the affected mode**. Workaround (boot at MAXN, then reapply) is awkward to automate headless, and the notes do not say headless is exempt.
- **Net:** the Orin NX remains the sweet spot on power, price and carrier availability — but its ROS-perception story is weaker than the page implied, and its headline TOPS carries a carrier condition.

### Also swept, corrected or confirmed
- **[Jetson Linux](entities/jetson-linux.md)** was the page yesterday's correction missed. It still headlined **"Current release — R36.5"** and repeated the exact claim fixed on the JetPack page — *"R36.x continues as the Orin line; R38.2 is Thor-only. The two lines are parallel, not sequential."* Rewritten around **R39.2** with an R36.5-vs-R39.2 table (kernel 5.15→6.8, rootfs 22.04→24.04, GCC 11.3→13.2, Orin-only→Orin **and** Thor), the JetPack→L4T mapping extended through 7.2, and the R38 section re-scoped — its remaining significance is that **Isaac ROS 4.x targets JetPack 7.1 = R38.4**, so Thor's supported robotics stack is *not* on r39.2.
- **Seeed BSP lag is ~2.5 weeks, not "weeks."** The vendor primary dates the J401 Orin NX 16 GB image **2026-06-18** against NVIDIA's 2026-06-01; yesterday's forum-sourced figure was 2026-06-30. Seeed's selector still defaults to R36.4.3 / JetPack 6.2.
- **Confirmed unchanged:** [DeepStream](entities/nvidia-deepstream.md) 9.1 (July 2026); [jetson-examples](entities/jetson-examples.md) JetPack 4.6→7.1 coverage; Thor T3000/T2000 GA Q1 2027, pricing undisclosed; AGX Orin 32 GB MAXN_SUPER 200→241 TOPS (corroborated by the r39.2 notes' own "adds support for Jetson AGX Orin 32GB Super Mode").
- **Still secondary-only:** **JetPack 7.2.1 (2026-08-12)**. It appears on no primary I could reach — NVIDIA's JetPack landing page is marketing copy and does not name it. Flagged as such rather than dropped, since the wiki quotes the date in three places.

### Methodological note
- The convention committed this morning was written from one incident. Applied to a whole cluster it found **three more errors of the same shape in a single pass** — two of them mine from yesterday, both from secondary coverage of a release whose primary I had already ingested but not read to the bottom. Reading a primary is not the same as reading *all* of it: yesterday's ingest took the release page's component table and stopped, and the release *notes* — a different document — held the known-issues section where the flashing behaviour actually lives.

## [2026-08-17] ingest | Zeroth M1 — a $2,499 home robot, and what its product page doesn't say
- Created [Zeroth M1 — product page](sources/zeroth-m1-product-page.md), [Zeroth Robotics](entities/zeroth-robotics.md), [Zeroth M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md). Updated [assistive robotics](concepts/robotics/assistive-robotics.md), [aging in place](concepts/robotics/aging-in-place.md), index.
- **What it is:** a **$2,499**, 494 mm, 2.8 kg, 20-DoF **hybrid biped/wheeled** home robot, sold as a pre-order. LDS LIDAR + iTOF depth + vision camera + IMU + 3-mic array (16 ft), ~2 h endurance, 80% charge in 1 h. Marketed for *"gentle fall detection, mobile safety checks, daily assistance, alerts, scam prevention, interactive learning for kids, pet behavior monitoring."* Siblings: **W1** $7,999 (28 kg outdoor/security), **Jupiter** $89,999 (human-scale humanoid), plus an unnamed "Humanoid — Coming Soon."
- **The two most informative numbers on the page are the two speeds.** **0.05 m/s bipedal** is ~3 m/min, about 1/25 of adult walking pace; **0.6 m/s wheeled** is ordinary indoor-robot speed. The biped is a demonstration mode and the wheels are the transport. For the safety pitch that distinction is load-bearing: crossing a 10 m room takes **~17 s wheeled and over three minutes walking**.
- **Filed in the social/monitoring band, not mobile manipulation.** At 2.8 kg with **no published payload, reach, or arm-DoF breakdown**, and nothing on the page describing lifting, carrying or fetching, it does not belong next to [Stretch](entities/stretch.md) ($29,950) or [OK-Robot](entities/ok-robot.md) — it belongs next to the companion robots in the [assistive-robotics](concepts/robotics/assistive-robotics.md) social category. **Its real contribution to this wiki is a price point, not a capability**: an order of magnitude below the in-home platforms the wiki benchmarks against.
- **What the page omits, checked by string search, and each omission matters:** no **compute specification of any kind** (no SoC, no on-device-vs-cloud split); no **privacy or data-handling statement** for an always-on camera + LIDAR + 16-foot mic array in a home; no Wi-Fi/connectivity spec; no ship date, deposit terms, refund policy or warranty; and **no accuracy figure, trial, or evaluation behind any safety claim.**
- **Secondary reporting says the M1 runs Google Gemini.** No Zeroth page mentions Gemini, cloud, or connectivity. Recorded as secondary and flagged: **this is the fact that decides whether the device is an assistive robot or a networked camera with legs**, since a cloud round-trip changes what "fall detection" means in a house with bad Wi-Fi.
- **The storefront is visibly unfinished, and I recorded the specifics rather than the impression** — the HTML meta description reads *"This is a product description for W1"*; the structured-data product name is literally `"M1 - 2499"` with empty description and category; schema.org says `InStock` under a "Reserve Now" button; the live Shopify theme is named `[dev] sainstore | M1 landing page | 20260804`; "Watch the full **videoo**" appears twice; the Jupiter page lists $89,999 but its button says $131,999; and **every About Us / Our Identity / Our Journey / Our Innovation / Press link returns HTTP 404**. Prices on this site are not yet a reliable class of claim.
- **Contradicted a marketing claim.** The site calls M1 *"one of the world's first embodied intelligence robots designed specifically for home environments."* The wiki's [domestic-robot precursors](concepts/robotics/assistive-robotics.md) and [long-term in-home deployments](syntheses/assistive/long-term-in-home-robot-deployments.md) records do not support that as a first-ness claim.
- **A note added to [aging in place](concepts/robotics/aging-in-place.md).** That page's standing observation was that mainstream 2023 elder-care guidance names *no* robots — only wearable alert systems. The M1 markets directly at that same safety niche, with no evidence behind it. The gap is now being filled by **marketing rather than by evidence**, which is arguably worse than the gap: an omission is a knowledge gap, an unevaluated fall-detector sold for elder safety is a claim a family may act on.
- **Applying this morning's convention:** the vendor page *is* the primary for price and dimensions, and I treated it as such. It is **not** primary for the compute stack, funding, or corporate structure — those are secondary-only here and marked as such on the entity page, since Zeroth's own company pages 404.

## [2026-08-17] query | "Other than NVIDIA, who benefits from the launch of robotics into the consumer market this year and next?"
- Synthesized from [UME](entities/ume.md), [FeeTech](entities/feetech.md), [Dynamixel](entities/dynamixel.md), [Vulcan Robotics](entities/vulcan-robotics.md), [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md), [Zeroth M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md), [Hailo](entities/hailo.md), [Raspberry Pi 5](entities/raspberry-pi-5.md), [Seeed Studio](entities/seeed-studio.md), [Orbbec](entities/orbbec.md), [LeRobot](entities/lerobot.md), [Stretch](entities/stretch.md), [XLeRobot](entities/xlerobot.md), [K-Scale Labs](entities/k-scale-labs.md), the [AI Index 2026](sources/stanford-hai-ai-index-2026.md) and the [LeRobot ICLR 2026 paper](sources/lerobot-iclr-2026-paper.md).
- Filed as **[Who benefits from consumer robotics in 2026–2027 (other than NVIDIA)](syntheses/society/consumer-robotics-value-chain.md)** — the wiki's first value-chain / market-structure page. `syntheses/society/` previously held only the two intelligence-north-star analyses.
- **First move was to adjust the premise.** The consumer "launch" this wiki documents is mostly **announcement, not shipment**: [M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md) is a pre-order on an unfinished storefront, [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md) has no published price and no live store, [K-Scale Labs](entities/k-scale-labs.md) shut down in late 2025. That splits beneficiaries onto **two clocks** — paid on *orders* vs paid on *retention* — which turned out to be the page's organizing result.
- **Best-evidenced beneficiary: actuator vendors**, on one number — [UME](sources/ume-paper.md)'s BOM is **86% quasi-direct-drive actuators** of $1,900. Actuators are the cost that scales with the product: compute is one module per robot, actuators are one per DoF, so the [M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md)'s **20 DoF is a 20-actuator order per unit**. [FeeTech](entities/feetech.md) is the specific position — ~3× cheaper than [Dynamixel](entities/dynamixel.md), which sets [SO-ARM101](entities/so-arm101.md) at €225 vs €670 for Koch-v1.1 and compounds into **50%+ of community LeRobotDataset contributions**.
- **Highest-leverage, thinnest-evidenced: whoever serves the model.** Three independent signs the consumer price point **cannot carry the compute** — the M1 publishes no compute spec at all and reportedly runs Gemini (secondary); [Vulcan](entities/vulcan-robotics.md) sells hardware **plus rented compute**, conceding the AI doesn't run on the robot; [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md) ships a **0.9 B policy beside a Pi 5 that cannot run it**. If that holds, the consumer robot is a **subscription-acquisition device** and revenue accrues per-robot-per-day to model servers, not at point of sale. Flagged as one confirmed business model + one secondary report + one inference, with the cheap falsifying test named: **establish what actually runs the M1's inference.**
- **Pushed back on two easy answers.** [LeRobot](entities/lerobot.md)/[Hugging Face](entities/hugging-face.md) is the obvious "picks and shovels" pick, but the wiki documents **mindshare, not monetization** — no mechanism here converts substrate position into consumer-wave revenue. And a cheap tier does **not** validate the research tier: [Stretch](entities/stretch.md) at $29,950 vs [M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md) at $2,499 for a device in a different capability band entirely (social/monitoring, no published payload or reach) makes the expensive platform look *overpriced* to buyers who can't tell the bands apart.
- **The capability gap does the real work.** **RLBench 89.4% sim vs [BEHAVIOR-1K](entities/behavior-benchmark.md) 12.4%** on realistic household tasks ([AI Index 2026](sources/stanford-hai-ai-index-2026.md)) means launches are running ahead of demonstrated capability, so retention-dependent players are exposed to a return wave that nothing in this wiki rules out — least of all a fall-detection claim sold for elder safety with **no accuracy figure, trial, or deployment evidence**.
- **Epistemic warning placed at the top of the page rather than the bottom**: this wiki holds **no revenue figures, unit forecasts, market sizing, or supply-chain data**. The analysis reads cost and dependency out of the technology stack, which shows who is *positioned* to capture value — never how much, or whether. Four "what would change this" items recorded, the cheapest being a single teardown: the wiki has BOMs for open platforms ([XLeRobot](entities/xlerobot.md) $660, UME $1,900) and **none for a commercial consumer product**.

## [2026-08-17] query | "Home AI platform" — agentic robot AI + home automation, multi-ecosystem support, trust vs authority
- Follow-on to the morning's [consumer-robotics value chain](syntheses/society/consumer-robotics-value-chain.md) query. Filed as **[The home AI platform — trust and authority](syntheses/agents/home-ai-platform-trust-and-authority.md)**.
- **First finding was a wiki gap, and it's a real one.** The smart-home platform ecosystem is **entirely absent**: no page on Matter, Thread, HomeKit, SmartThings, Alexa, Nest, or Ring, and no ingested source on any of them. Apparent grep hits were substring false positives ("du**ring**", "**matter**", "**Alex**ander"). The page marks its smart-home claims as uncited rather than blending them with sourced material.
- **Naming.** Consumer category will be **"home AI platform"**; the load-bearing name is the **household world model** — floorplan, objects, routines, people, state over time. **"Personal AI" is wrong**: the unit is the household, and multi-tenancy (children, elders, guests, cleaners, asymmetric consent) is both the hardest problem and the whole privacy exposure. Naming relocates the moat (device lock-in is re-buyable; **world-model lock-in is historical**) and the regulation ([world-model governance](concepts/safety/world-model-governance.md) targets "persistent spatial profiles," i.e. the model holder, not the hardware seller).
- **The fusion works on rate and breaks on idempotency.** [Control abstraction levels](concepts/robotics/control-abstraction-levels.md): levels 2 and 4 sidestep the 83 Hz problem by making the model *write* the controller rather than *be* it, so a sub-Hz declarative home layer composes fine. But **Matter's multi-admin model works because device state has a well-defined join** — two controllers asking for "on" yields on — and **robot state has no join**. Two ecosystems asking for different rooms is undefined, and undefined here means a physical object in motion.
- **The multi-ecosystem answer already exists in the wiki, twice, independently.** [AgenticROS](entities/agenticros.md) serves **six agent platforms** from one robot via **typed capability manifests** (`interruptible`, `blocks_base`) with **`/estop` bypassing the AI**; [ros2-mcp-server](entities/ros2-mcp-server.md) reaches the same shape with config-driven tool filtering, a `{status,reason,observation}` envelope and out-of-band stop. **The integration surface is a typed capability manifest over MCP, authored by the robot vendor** — the only shape that lets one vendor serve Google *and* Amazon *and* Apple without three firmwares and three safety cases. Recorded as a **weak convergence, not a standard**: both are small, AgenticROS is anonymous and nav-only.
- **Multi-homing splits by layer.** Yes at the command surface (cheap, demonstrated); vendor-owned at skill/policy; **no at the household world model** — duplicated sensing, divergent state, no merge semantics. So the platform fight is **not about the robot**: vendors will support every assistant because that's cheap and it isn't the asset. The contest is **which ecosystem's model the robot reports observations into.**
- **Trust and authority separate at three boundaries, not one.** (1) **Authority is a position in the stack**, not a permission — "access level is a core part of the system," influence varies by orders of magnitude with access. The plausible split gives ecosystems Level 3 and keeps Levels 1–2 with the vendor — but the wiki flags this boundary as **load-bearing by accident**, resting on models being unable to drive joints, and it "erodes as level-1 capability improves." Perceptual access counts too: a **compass was the largest single performance lever**, above most reasoning-budget effects. (2) **Trust is liability, and it cannot yet be assigned** — governance asks for a log of "what it perceived, the state it inferred, the action it took," but for an end-to-end policy that state is a **latent vector with no committed semantics**; "logging it is easy, reading it after an incident is the unsolved part." **Authority partitions cleanly today; liability does not** — predicting that incumbents with legal departments demand Level-3-and-above access for *defensibility* rather than safety. (3) **Consent is household-level**, and the people observed most (children, elders, visitors) consented least.
- **The e-stop is the anchor** and has no smart-home precedent — the most compact statement of why this is not just an extension of Nest and Ring.
- **Load-bearing caveat carried into the page**: the enforcement layer these boundaries assume is largely aspirational. [Guardrails](syntheses/agents/guardrails-for-robot-agents.md) — the execution rail ships empty, MCP allowlist is the only thing playing the role; [Nav2 BTs](sources/nav2-behavior-trees-docs.md) qualify it (a real rail ships, **scoped to navigation**, nothing equivalent for manipulation); [NemoClaw](entities/nemoclaw.md) is the one shipped commercial wrapper and is early-preview; NVIDIA's [safety recipe](sources/nvidia-safety-recipe-agentic-ai.md) had the right architecture and was deprecated 2026-04-22.
- **Highest-value ingest to ground the missing half: the Matter specification** — specifically what its multi-admin model guarantees and whether any of it survives non-idempotent state.

## [2026-08-17] ingest | Matter 1.4 Core Specification — the primary that falsified my own claim
- Created [Matter 1.4 Core Specification](sources/matter-1-4-core-specification.md) (CSA Doc 23-27349, 2024-11-04, 1,173 pp; PDF in `raw/`), [Matter](entities/matter.md), [Connectivity Standards Alliance](entities/connectivity-standards-alliance.md). Corrected [the home AI platform synthesis](syntheses/agents/home-ai-platform-trust-and-authority.md). Closed the top backlog item and opened its successor.
- **The wiki's first home-automation primary of any kind.** Ingested for one reason: this morning's synthesis asserted a claim about Matter's multi-admin semantics that rested entirely on my own reading of a specification nobody here had read.

### It falsified the mechanism
- **What I wrote**: *"Matter's multi-admin model works because device state is effectively a lattice with a well-defined join: two controllers both asking a bulb to be on yields *on*. Conflicts resolve by construction."*
- **What §7.5.3 actually says**: *"**Most cluster data instances are accessible regardless of the accessing fabric.**"* Fabric-scoping — the isolation mechanism — is explicitly limited to *"list of fabric-scoped structs"* and *"fabric-sensitive event"*, i.e. **ACLs, bindings, group keys: configuration, not state**. A light's `OnOff` is one shared value every commissioned fabric can read and write.
- **And there is no conflict resolution at all.** **"arbitrat" appears zero times in 1,173 pages**; all eight occurrences of "conflict" concern DNS-SD name collisions and ephemeral node IDs. Two ecosystems writing the same attribute is not an error, not a conflict, and not arbitrated by any rule in the standard.
- **The conclusion survived in a stronger form.** Matter's model doesn't extend to robots — but not because robot state lacks a join. **Matter never solved arbitration; it externalized the problem to the triviality of the state.** A bulb toggling between two admins is an annoyance, cheaply re-set, physically inconsequential — exactly the property a mobile actuated robot lacks. The analogy fails deeper than I claimed: there is no mechanism to borrow, only an assumption that stops holding.

### It corroborated the other half, unexpectedly
- The synthesis predicted vendors would need to invent a manifest bounding what each ecosystem may touch. **They already did, and it is called the Access Restriction List** (§6.6): *"a per-fabric Access Restriction List (ARL), **which is set by the device**… Access Restriction Entries, which identify the attributes, commands and events on specific endpoint clusters **which are not accessible on a given fabric**."*
- It **overrides the ecosystem's own administrator** — an ACL entry granting Operate over everything still yields **`ACCESS_RESTRICTED`**, because the ARL is *"a subsequent overriding of an initial privilege granted."* Discoverable pre-commissioning via `CommissioningARL`, negotiable via `ReviewFabricRestrictions`.
- **So the vendor-authored capability manifest is not a prediction — it is consumer-scale precedent**, expressed as *data* rather than as an API convention, with a certification story attached. That is the likelier commercial shape for the robot case too. [AgenticROS](entities/agenticros.md)'s `interruptible`/`blocks_base` and [ros2-mcp-server](entities/ros2-mcp-server.md)'s tool filtering reached the same shape independently and later.

### Also recorded
- **Fabric = ecosystem**, rooted in a Root CA issuing NOCs, fabrics *"unrelated by any common roots of trust."* System-model minima: **≥4 ACL entries per fabric** (5 fabrics ⇒ ≥20), ≥3 group keys per fabric, ≥9-path Read Interactions per fabric.
- **Deny-by-default access control** over five strictly nested privileges — `View` → `ProxyView`/`Operate` → `Manage` → `Administer` — matched **per fabric**, so an administrator in one ecosystem cannot grant privileges in another.
- **Fabric Synchronization** and **Joint Fabric** (both new in 1.4): ecosystems sharing devices, with *"Each ecosystem SHALL independently ask the user for consent"*, an **Anchor CA** trusted across fabrics, and a Joint Fabric Datastore Cluster.
- **Matter has no robot device type**, and nothing in 1.4 contemplates a mobile actuated node. It also says nothing about **human** multi-tenancy — its subjects are nodes and certificates, not people.
- **Version honesty**: 1.4 is one major version behind (1.5/1.5.1 exist; 1.5 adds cameras and closures). 1.4 is the newest whose PDF is **directly retrievable** — CSA's download-request page 403s unauthenticated fetchers, and 1.5's PDF was not linkable. Flagged on every page rather than quietly presented as current.
- **Extraction**: direct `curl` of the uploads path; `pypdf` for text (2.48 M chars). The gated download page was never needed.

### Methodological
- **Third correction-of-my-own-work today, and the first one I went looking for on purpose.** The morning's convention says ingest the primary when a claim will be quoted in a decision; this claim was going to be quoted in an architecture argument, I flagged it as the top backlog item at the time of writing, and the check paid out twice — one falsification, one corroboration stronger than the original prediction. **Naming the falsifying test when writing the claim is what made this cheap**, and that is the part worth repeating.

## [2026-08-17] query | "Analyze DimensionalOS through the lens of a home AI platform"
- Applied this morning's [home AI platform framework](syntheses/agents/home-ai-platform-trust-and-authority.md) to [DimOS](entities/dimos.md). Filed as **[DimOS as a home-AI substrate](syntheses/agents/dimos-as-home-ai-substrate.md)**. First application of the framework to a named stack, and the first test of whether it does any work.
- **It does. The split is clean: DimOS owns the asset and lacks the authority model.**
- **It already implements the household world model.** `dimos/memory2` — spatio-temporal SQLite with embeddings, *"spatio-temporal RAG, dynamic memory, object localization and permanence"*, queryable/composable streams, SVG over a global map — plus column-carving voxel maps, premap + GTSAM pose-graph relocalization, and `tag_location(name)` binding names to places. **The strategically loaded part: it is Apache-2.0 and the asset is a SQLite file on your disk.** What the incumbents would hold as a cloud service, DimOS hands to the owner. If the moat is world-model lock-in, this is the anti-lock-in position — and nothing in Dimensional's own materials markets it that way.
- **It is accidentally the most auditable architecture in the wiki.** [World-model governance](concepts/safety/world-model-governance.md) wants a log of *"what the system perceived, the state it inferred, and the action it took"*, and the wiki's standing objection is that for an end-to-end policy the inferred state is *"a latent vector with no committed semantics."* **That objection does not apply here**: DimOS's inferred state is a voxel map, a pose graph, an A* path, and `dimos --replay run` re-runs the full SLAM/costmap/A* stack on a recorded session with no hardware. The reason is the same one that makes it look weak on capability — **no VLA in the control loop at all**. For a category facing a liability regime that trade may be correct, and it is the strongest argument for DimOS its own docs never make. **It is also fragile: the property comes from the absence of a learned policy, and `dimos dataprep` already feeds LeRobot v3.0.**
- **The authority model is inverted, and that is the real gap.** [Matter](entities/matter.md)'s ARL is **vendor-authored, per-fabric, deny-by-default**, overriding the ecosystem's own administrator. DimOS's `@skill` is **allow-by-decoration**: discovered at startup by RPC introspection, republished over MCP to *any* attaching client, globally, with no per-client scoping. The [DimOS page](entities/dimos.md) praises discovery because *"the manifest cannot drift from the code"* — through this lens the same property is the liability, because **exposure becomes a property of the code rather than of the relationship**. There is no way to express "this client may not do that."
- **And the surface reaches low.** Shipped skills include **`relative_move(forward, left, degrees)`** — direct base motion, not a goal to a pretrained policy. So the framework's predicted split (ecosystem gets Level 3, vendor keeps 1–2) **fails here**, and the de-facto safety boundary [control abstraction levels](concepts/robotics/control-abstraction-levels.md) identifies — the pretrained-policy layer — is simply absent.
- **Asymmetry worth checking in the tree**: [AgenticROS](entities/agenticros.md) ships `blocks_base`, `interruptible`, and an `/estop` bypassing the AI; **DimOS's ingested evidence shows none of the three**, on a codebase an order of magnitude larger. Recorded as a statement about the wiki's summary of a 277 MB repo, **not** a proof about the code — and named as the page's highest-value open question, since it is a `grep` away for anyone with the tree.
- **Trust: right capabilities, wrong defaults, twice.** Default LLM is cloud `gpt-5.6-luna`; local Ollama exists but is not default. **dimTELE** — *"the robot dials out to a hosted broker, so no inbound ports need opening"* — is excellent NAT engineering and, as trust architecture, **a persistent outbound tunnel from inside the home to a vendor's server**, which is also the company's clearest business model. No multi-tenancy anywhere: `human_input: In[str]` is one undifferentiated channel.
- **Placement**: RTX 3000+/8 GB VRAM, AGX Orin tested, **Orin Nano experimental** — a $2,499 [Zeroth M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md) cannot run it. DimOS is the **layer beneath** a consumer home-AI platform, not the platform, and its open-core-plus-hosted-connectivity model sits exactly in the [value chain](syntheses/society/consumer-robotics-value-chain.md)'s Tier 2 alongside [Vulcan](entities/vulcan-robotics.md)'s rented inference.
- **The one change that would make it credible as a home-AI substrate**: an ARL-equivalent — per-client, deny-by-default restriction on which `@skill`s are reachable, declared separately from the implementing code. Matter shows the shape and shows it ships at consumer scale; DimOS's discovery mechanism is the piece that would have to give.

## [2026-08-17] overview | "How this wiki discriminates" — the ten strongest axes, added to overview.md
- Query: *"list the top ten strongest axes in this wiki."* Filed as a new **[overview.md](overview.md)** section rather than a synthesis page — it is orientation material about *how to read* the wiki, so it belongs beside "what's in it," between the themes and the conventions.
- **Ranked by four tests**: recurs across many pages, discriminates between things that otherwise look alike, is anchored in numbers rather than opinion, and has changed at least one conclusion here.
- **The ten**: (1) [control abstraction level](concepts/robotics/control-abstraction-levels.md); (2) [statistical power / N](syntheses/platforms/vla-success-rate-audit.md); (3) [control rate](syntheses/platforms/control-rate-ladder.md); (4) benchmark-vs-deployment gap; (5) power envelope; (6) cost ladder; (7) [code-as-policy vs end-to-end policy](concepts/agents/code-as-policy.md); (8) [compute placement](syntheses/agents/on-device-and-on-robot-agents.md); (9) [prevention/detection/intervention](syntheses/platforms/prevention-detection-intervention.md); (10) [readability vs embodiment-agnosticism](syntheses/agents/action-representation-languages.md).
- **Three flagged as prerequisites** because they change how everything else reads: axis 1 is **non-monotonic** (capability *inverts* across levels, so an eval claim without its level is not a claim), axis 2 is **retroactive** (it re-scores comparisons already filed), and axis 3 explains **structure not speed** (the ~100× rate gap is bridged architecturally, by making the model write the controller rather than be it).
- **Nine of ten are quantitative and orthogonal to marketing** — that is the property that makes them useful. The one qualitative entry earns its place by holding constant across four years and every stack ingested.
- **Also recorded: the five weak axes**, in a warning callout, because they are where the wiki will mislead a reader — **economics** (no revenue/unit/market/supply-chain data anywhere), **human factors and multi-tenancy** (no ingested source at all — a literature gap, not just a wiki gap), **long-horizon reliability** (nearly everything is single-session), **safety certification** (ISO 13482 documented as a pathway with nothing measured against it), and **home automation** (one source, [Matter 1.4](sources/matter-1-4-core-specification.md)).
- **Refreshed the Quick stats table**, which was three months stale and understated the wiki by a factor of ~2.6: **383 → 1,000 pages** (sources 145→414, entities 164→412, concepts 26→86, syntheses 44→83). Kept the old column alongside the new. Sources and entities grew in step, which is the ingest pattern working as designed — a single ingest touches 5–15 pages, so neither outruns the other.

## [2026-08-17] correction | DimOS tree check — the arbitration claim was wrong, the authority claim was right
- Resolved the standing hedge on [DimOS as a home-AI substrate](syntheses/agents/dimos-as-home-ai-substrate.md), which the page itself named as its highest-value open question. Checked via the **GitHub tree API (2,775 paths) plus raw file reads** — no clone of the 277 MB repo needed.
- **The page said arbitration was absent. It is not, and the mechanism is better than the one I compared it against.** `dimos/agents/capabilities.py` implements a **`CapabilityRegistry`** — *"capability registry for skill-level mutual exclusion."* Skills **declare** occupancy via **`@skill(uses=[...])`**; the MCP server acquires before every `tools/call`. Holds are keyed by **per-invocation token** rather than tool name, so a stale teardown cannot free a live hold and a same-tool re-acquire is a **takeover**, while different tools sharing a capability **conflict**. Acquire is **atomic all-or-nothing**, try-lock by default, with an optional timeout that waits only on `instant` holders (`background` holders *"run until explicitly stopped, so refuse instead"*). On conflict the server refuses — *"Cannot start X: capability Y is held by Z"* — and hands the decision back to the agent. **That is a real preemption primitive, and more developed than [AgenticROS](entities/agenticros.md)'s `blocks_base` boolean.**
- **But the vocabulary has one word in it**: *"today the only declared capability is `CAP_MOVEMENT`."* The base is arbitrated; **arms, cameras and the speaker are not**. Mechanism sound, essentially unpopulated.
- **The authority claim held, and now has a citation.** `mcp_server.py`'s only middleware is CORS — **`allow_origins=["*"]`, `allow_methods=["POST","GET"]`, `allow_headers=["*"]`** — with no `Depends` / `HTTPBearer` / `APIKey` / `Security` on either `POST /mcp` or `GET /mcp`. **No per-client scoping**: any attaching client gets every `@skill`. No `estop` / `emergency` / `interlock` / `deadman` path anywhere in the tree (nearest: `core/coordination/watchdog_main.py`).
- **The sharpened version of the page's thesis**: DimOS solved the **concurrency** half of multi-homing and not the **authority** half. The registry says *not at the same time*; [Matter](entities/matter.md)'s ARL says *not by you*. Those are different problems and DimOS has exactly one of them.
- **One framing softened.** The page called the manifest purely "discovered, not declared." **`uses=[...]` is a declared field**, so DimOS declares *occupancy* and discovers *existence* — which matters constructively: the decorator already carries per-skill metadata, so an authority field could sit beside `uses=` without redesigning anything. The gap is not a missing manifest format; it is that the manifest has no notion of **who is asking**.
- **Incidental, and a trap for anyone grepping**: **"security" in the DimOS tree means the surveillance application** — `experimental/security_demo/`, `unitree_go2_security.py` — not access control. Searching the word finds the opposite of what a reader expects, which is how the original summary-level check missed the real answer.
- Also recorded `SkillResult` (`agents/skill_result.py`): typed `error_code` returns replacing free-form strings, with domain `Literal` aliases constraining which codes a skill may emit.
- Propagated to the [synthesis](syntheses/agents/dimos-as-home-ai-substrate.md) (correction callout, two verdict-table rows, two open questions closed), the [DimOS entity](entities/dimos.md) (two new callouts), and the [source page](sources/dimos-github.md) (dated addendum — the repo is living, so the 2026-08-13 reading stands as what the docs said and the addendum records what the tree says).
- **Matter 1.5 attempted and failed**: five direct-URL patterns probed against the csa-iot uploads path, all **404**. The trick that retrieved 1.4 does not generalize; 1.5 needs the gated form. Noted on the backlog item rather than left as a silent non-attempt.
- **Methodological**: this is the fourth correction-of-my-own-work today and the second sought deliberately. The page named the check, the check took three API calls, and it falsified half the verdict table **two hours after publication**. Naming the falsifying test is cheap; not naming it is how a wrong claim survives.

## [2026-08-17] ingest | Matter 1.6 — the full four-document set, and the arbitration gap is structural
- User supplied **Matter 1.6** (2026-06-16) through CSA's gated download form after this morning's five direct-URL probes all 404'd. Four documents, all now in `raw/`: **[Core](sources/matter-1-6-core-specification.md)** (1,335 pp), **[Application Cluster](sources/matter-1-6-application-cluster-specification.md)** (982 pp), **[Device Library](sources/matter-1-6-device-library.md)** (229 pp), **[Standard Namespaces](sources/matter-1-6-standard-namespaces.md)** (71 pp). First time the wiki has held a multi-document standard in full rather than one document of it.

### The question it was ingested to answer, answered
- The [home AI platform](syntheses/agents/home-ai-platform-trust-and-authority.md) page asked whether Matter's missing cross-controller arbitration was **structural or incidental** — the test being whether device classes that physically move would bring arbitration with them. **They did not.** `arbitrat` and `interlock` appear **zero times across all four 1.6 documents**, two major versions after 1.4, *after* adding **Closures** and with a **Robotic Vacuum Cleaner** in the library. **§7.5.3 is verbatim unchanged** — "most cluster data instances are accessible regardless of the accessing fabric," isolation still limited to lists of structs and fabric-sensitive events.
- **CSA is not unaware of the problem; it solved it exactly where it chose to.** All nine `conflict` hits in the 1.6 core were read individually: five are identifier collisions carried from 1.4, one is **`BusyWithOtherAdmin`** for two *commissioners* colliding, and one is the **Ecosystem Information Cluster** doing "conflict resolution between multiple sources of the name and location data." Mutual exclusion for sessions, reconciliation for metadata, **nothing for commands**. That is a design decision.

### The correction — and it is the same error shape as this morning's
- **"Matter has no robot device type" was wrong.** The [Device Library](sources/matter-1-6-device-library.md) defines **§12 Robotic Device Types**, first entry **Robotic Vacuum Cleaner `0x0074`** (mandatory RVC Run Mode + RVC Operational State, optional **Service Area**). I asserted that absence from the **Core Specification, which is not the document that defines device types** — the same failure as the JetPack release-page-vs-release-notes error earlier today: **asserting an absence from a document that would not have contained the thing.** Corrected on [Matter](entities/matter.md) and the [1.4 source page](sources/matter-1-4-core-specification.md); the [CSA entity](entities/connectivity-standards-alliance.md) now carries a table of what each of the four documents defines, precisely so the mistake is harder to repeat.

### The finding that reframes the whole reading
- **Matter's safety model is device-side refusal, not controller arbitration.** The device unilaterally ignores or rejects commands on its own local sensors and state and reports why: Window Covering's **`SafetyStatusBitmap`** — `RemoteLockout` (*"Movement commands are ignored… e.g. **not granted authorization**"*), `StopInput` (*"Local safety sensor… preventing movements (e.g. **Safety EU Standard EN60335**)"*), `ManualOperation`, `TamperDetection`, `MotorJammed`, `ThermalProtection`; **maintenance mode** (*"all commands… must be ignored"*, answer `BUSY`); **`CommandInvalidInState`** for *"regulatory or manufacturer-imposed safety and security requirements"*; **EVSE lockout** until latching conditions are met. Note that `RemoteLockout` puts **an authority statement inside a safety bitmap**, and `StopInput` is the only place in 1,335+982+229+71 pages where an external safety standard is named as a behavioural cause.
- **[DimOS](entities/dimos.md)'s `CapabilityRegistry` reached the same answer independently** — *"Cannot start X: capability Y is held by Z."* Two systems, no shared lineage, both concluding the thing holding the actuator should be the thing that says no.
- **But refusal is only a safe default when the null action is safe.** A window covering that refuses to move is safe; **a robot that refuses to move may be blocking a doorway.** That is a property of the device class, not the protocol, and it is now the sharpest reason Matter's model does not extend to a home robot.

### Standard Namespaces — the household world model's schema, published
- 28 semantic-tag namespaces, including **Common Area** (Bedroom, Ensuite, GuestBathroom, Attic, Hallway, Boxroom…), **Common Landmark** (Bed, **Crib**, **Cradle**, **HighChair**, Toilet, Shower, **PetBed**, **LitterBox**, ScratchingPost, Nightstand…), and **Identified Human Activity** — Unknown, **`0x01 Fall`**, Sleeping, Walking, Workout, Sitting, Standing, Dancing, PackageDelivery, PackageRetrieval.
- **This cuts two ways.** It **weakens the world-model moat** the platform pages argue over, because a shared ontology is what makes a household model portable between ecosystems. And it **converts the privacy argument from hypothetical to itemised**: crossing three namespaces, the standard vocabulary already distinguishes **who lives there** (Crib/HighChair → infant; LitterBox/ScratchingPost → cat; GuestBedroom → visitors), **where the private spaces are** (Bedroom, Ensuite, Shower, Toilet), and **what people are doing in them** (Sleeping, Walking, **Fall**) — **no camera and no name required**. That is exactly the harm shape [world-model governance](concepts/safety/world-model-governance.md) describes, now with a published vocabulary behind it.
- **`Fall` is now a cross-vendor interoperable signal**, while the [Zeroth M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md) still markets fall detection with no accuracy figure and no trial. **Standardising how a claim is reported does nothing to standardise whether it is true**, and no conformance bar for detection quality was found. Filed on [aging in place](concepts/robotics/aging-in-place.md), where the standing observation was that the gap is being filled by marketing rather than evidence — this widens it.
- Also recorded: full version timeline from the spec's own revision history (1.0 2022-09-23 → **1.6 2026-06-16**, ~two releases a year), **89 device types** enumerated, ARL mentions **grown** 5→11 with `ReviewFabricRestrictions` 1→17, and the RVC's `CannotReachTargetArea` error — a robot reporting semantic navigation failure against a named area model.

## [2026-08-17] correction | Matter camera clusters — "no arbitration" was a vocabulary artifact
- Follow-up grep on a document already in `raw/`, prompted by noticing that the first pass through the [Application Cluster spec](sources/matter-1-6-application-cluster-specification.md) searched for **safety and refusal** semantics and skipped **camera and privacy** semantics. It changed a headline claim published earlier the same day.
- **The claim**: `arbitrat` = 0 across all four Matter 1.6 documents, therefore Matter defines no cross-fabric conflict resolution. **The problem**: that searched for the word *I* would use. **CSA's term is "conflict resolution."**
- **§11.2.1.2.2 of the Application Cluster spec is titled "Multiple Stream Resource Conflict Resolution."** For camera streams *"shared among clients (potentially in different fabrics)"* it defines: an administrator-configurable **`SetStreamPriorities`** ranked list; a **mandatory reuse rule** — matching parameters ⇒ *"the camera SHALL reuse the existing one"*; and an **incumbent-protected, newcomer-rejected** policy — a new request that would violate an existing stream's minimum configuration *"SHALL be rejected with a FAILED notification."*
- **The surviving claim is narrower and better than the original: Matter has join semantics for sensing and none for actuation.** Two fabrics requesting the same stream get **literally the same stream**; two fabrics writing the same attribute get last-write-wins. **Sensing composes; actuation does not.** That is a coherent design, and it is a sharper reason the model fails for a home robot than "no arbitration anywhere" was — **a robot is mostly actuation**. Still true and unchanged: **nothing anywhere resolves two fabrics issuing competing movement commands.**
- **Camera privacy is first-class and hardware-backed**, which the first pass also missed. **`HardPrivacyModeOn`** — *"controlled via a **physical button or switch**… TRUE indicates that **all streams are currently paused**"* — plus soft per-usage recording and livestream modes. Enforcement is concrete: active WebRTC transports *"SHALL terminate the session"* with reason `PrivacyMode`, and `CaptureSnapshot` fails `INVALID_IN_STATE`. **That physical switch is the strongest device-side veto in the standard — an e-stop for data, on a standard with no e-stop for motion.**
- **What is absent**: `retention` = 0 hits, and no deletion or export requirements. **Matter's privacy is capture-time control, not lifecycle governance** — it says when a camera may look and nothing about what happens to what it saw. Relevant to [world-model governance](concepts/safety/world-model-governance.md)'s "persistent spatial profiles," which are precisely a lifecycle concern.
- Also recorded the **Zone Management Cluster** (§2.14) — user- or manufacturer-defined regions of interest plus **Triggers**, the spatial primitive that would bind an [Identified Human Activity](sources/matter-1-6-standard-namespaces.md) tag such as `Fall` to a place.
- **Methodological — third lexical blind spot today, and they rhyme.** DimOS's "security" means *surveillance*, not access control. Matter's device types live in the *Device Library*, not the Core spec. Matter's arbitration is called *conflict resolution*. **Keyword absence is only as strong as the vocabulary guess behind it**, and every one of today's absence-claims that failed, failed this way. The fix is not more greps; it is reading a document's own table of contents before concluding something is missing from it.

## [2026-08-23] ingest | GraspGen-X (CVPR 2026) + Nori Robotics NORI A3
Two unrelated sources ingested together; each fills a hole the other doesn't touch.

**GraspGen-X** — project page requested, but the page is a summary of a document, so the [arXiv primary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00998) (27 pp.) was pulled and read. Worth it: the project page and the paper disagree on the dataset size three ways.
- Created [GraspGen-X: Cross-Embodiment 6-DOF Diffusion-based Grasping](sources/graspgenx-paper.md)
- New concept: [6-DOF grasp generation](concepts/robotics/six-dof-grasp-generation.md) — the wiki has cited grasping in ~20 pages and never defined it as a module
- New entities: [GraspGen / GraspGen-X](entities/graspgen.md), [Adithyavairavan Murali](entities/adithyavairavan-murali.md)
- Updated [AgileX Piper](entities/agilex-piper.md) (now the wiki's most-measured affordable arm for grasping, from two non-meeting directions), [Unitree G1](entities/unitree-g1.md) (3-finger hand as a grasping embodiment; the swept-volume representation's stated blind spot on its asymmetric thumb), [cuRobo](entities/curobo.md) (IK rejection as the *selection* mechanism, inverting the 5-DoF warning already on that page), [NVIDIA](entities/nvidia.md), [Motion planning](concepts/robotics/motion-planning.md), [Soft-prompt cross-embodiment conditioning](concepts/learning/soft-prompt-cross-embodiment.md)
- **Contradiction flagged, inside the authors' own abstract.** The abstract says "trained with a large-scale dataset of 2 Billion grasps"; Appendix F.1 says 2 B is the **post-CVPR checkpoint** and every published number comes from the **350 M** CVPR model. The project page's abstract carries a *third* figure, 395 M. Scope loss of the exact kind `CLAUDE.md` warns about in secondaries — occurring here in a primary.
- **Convergence with existing wiki work.** GraspGen-X's post-CVPR release adds a **Grasp Mixture-of-Experts** (PCA oriented-bounding-box sampler unioned with the diffusion sampler) because *"user feedback highlights a need for top-down grasps."* The [5-DoF RoboTwin plan](syntheses/projects/five-dof-arms-in-robotwin.md) derived the same constraint from arm kinematics in Aug 2026. Noted there: it weakens the novelty of that plan's "bias toward top-down" half and leaves the radial-lateral and constraint-manifold halves untouched. Plan retargeted, not invalidated.

**Nori Robotics** — vendor page requested; the [YC S26 profile](sources/nori-robotics-yc-profile.md) ingested alongside it as a second primary because it carries the compute, the 55 kg reconciliation, and the business thesis, none of which appear on the storefront.
- Created [NORI A3 product site](sources/nori-robotics-site.md), [Y Combinator company profile](sources/nori-robotics-yc-profile.md)
- New entities: [Nori Robotics](entities/nori-robotics.md), [NORI A3](entities/nori-a3.md)
- Updated [Raspberry Pi 5](entities/raspberry-pi-5.md), [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md), [Zeroth M1](entities/zeroth-m1.md), [Consumer robotics value chain](syntheses/society/consumer-robotics-value-chain.md)
- **Contradiction flagged and resolved from primaries.** Secondary coverage reports the A3 "can lift 55 kg" against the vendor's own "1.5 kg payload per-arm." The YC page lists both: **55 kg is the torso lift column**, 1.5 kg is the hands. Textbook scope loss; the vendor page is right and the secondary dropped what the number was bound to.
- **Contradiction, unresolved.** Vendor page says "ships fall 2026"; secondaries and the YC profile report a first unit already deployed (21 July 2026). Probably an early hand-built unit ahead of the general window; not resolvable from either primary.
- **The compute is the finding.** A Pi 5 **4 GB** is the entire onboard computer of a $1,688 bimanual robot. [Sourccey](entities/sourccey.md) uses the same board and cannot run its own 0.9 B [X-VLA](entities/x-vla.md) policies onboard; 4 GB also forecloses the 8 GB [Hailo](entities/hailo.md) AI HAT+ 2. So "Nori Lab, the laptop app" is where inference lives and the A3 is a **thin client** — unremarkable at the price, undisclosed in both primaries.
- **Partially falsifies the [value-chain](syntheses/society/consumer-robotics-value-chain.md) premise**, which was written when nothing in this tier had a price *and* a shipment. Nori has both, at the lowest price in the tier. Noted inline there rather than rewritten.

## [2026-08-23] research | GraspGen-X gripper onboarding — the repo is live and ships a wizard
- Question: what does it take to generate a swept-volume cube for the [SO-101](entities/so-arm101.md)?
- **The premise of this morning's backlog item was slightly wrong.** The swept volume is not computed from a URDF — `scripts/gripper_config_wizard.py` **auto-guesses then hand-annotates** it in a six-step Viser GUI. Recipe and config schema recorded on [GraspGen](entities/graspgen.md).
- [NVlabs/GraspGenX](https://github.com/NVlabs/GraspGenX) is public (Apache-2.0, last push 2026-07-14); checkpoints on HuggingFace; ~10 curated gripper configs ship as a separate dataset. **No SO-101, no arm under $1k anywhere in that library**, and the maintainers invite PRs.
- README states the released checkpoint as **2 B grasps / 32 procedural grippers in 6 kinematic families / 8 K+ objects** — the "6 kinematic families" figure is not in the paper, which describes three categories. Recorded on the entity.
- Three failure modes predicted from reading the wizard source and verified against the SO-101 URDF (single actuated `gripper` joint, one moving link `moving_jaw_so101_v1_link`): the full-arm URDF auto-types wrong, `detect_finger_geoms()` returns a single link so the auto-guessed box is **degenerate**, and one actuated joint auto-types `parallel_2f` for what is a revolute jaw. All three written into [backlog](backlog.md) with fixes.
- Also established: **the wizard runs on WSL** (geometry + browser GUI, no CUDA), unlike the [RoboTwin 5-DoF plan](syntheses/projects/five-dof-arms-in-robotwin.md) which is hard-blocked on rendering.

## [2026-08-23] ingest | Where Security Fits in an AI Agent Stack (NVIDIA)
- Created [Where Security Fits in an AI Agent Stack](sources/nvidia-where-security-fits-agent-stack.md) — curled and extracted the article rather than summarizing the summary; the AI-generated summary at the top of the page omits the four security profiles and the physical-systems carve-out.
- New entity: [NVIDIA OpenShell](entities/nvidia-openshell.md)
- Updated [AI guardrails](concepts/safety/ai-guardrails.md), [Robot security](concepts/robotics/robot-security.md), [Guardrails for robot agents](syntheses/agents/guardrails-for-robot-agents.md), [Home AI platform](syntheses/agents/home-ai-platform-trust-and-authority.md), [NemoClaw](entities/nemoclaw.md), [Hermes Agent](entities/hermes-agent.md), [NVIDIA](entities/nvidia.md)
- **This corrects the frame of an existing synthesis, not just adds to it.** The [safety layer cake](syntheses/agents/guardrails-for-robot-agents.md) sorted five layers by *what they guard*. NVIDIA's criterion — *"a control that the agent can decline to invoke is not an effective security control"* — sorts them by *whether the agent can bypass them*, and on that axis **layers 2–5 are all advisory and only the physical interlock is a boundary**.
